Towards a noncommutative theory of Cowen-Douglas class of noncommuting operators Thanks: P. Deb gratefully acknowledges the postdoctoral fellowship supported by Israel Science Foundation (ISF) Grant No. 2123/17 in the Department of Mathematics at Ben-Gurion University of the Negev, as well as the professional development fund (PDA) and the initial research grant (IRG) provided by IIIT-Delhi, for supporting his research. Thanks: V. Vinnikov acknowledges Israel Science Foundation (ISF) Grant No. 2123/17 for supporting this work. He would also like to thank Milken Families Foundation Chair in Mathematics for a partial support of his research.
Prahllad Deb, Victor Vinnikov

Abstract
. The classical Cowen-Douglas class of (commuting tuples of) operators possessing an open set of (joint) eigenvalues of finite constant multiplicity was introduced by Cowen and Douglas, generalizing the backward shifts. Their unitary equivalence classes are determined by the equivalence classes of certain hermitian holomorphic vector bundles associated with them on this set. This article develops a free noncommutative analogue of Cowen-Douglas theory to explore the notion of vector bundles in the setting of free noncommutative function theory. We define the noncommutative Cowen-Douglas class using matricial joint eigenvalues, as envisioned by Taylor, and show via the Taylor-Taylor series that the associated joint eigenspaces naturally form such a vector bundle, what we call a noncommutative hermitian holomorphic vector bundle. A key result is that the unitary equivalence class of a tuple in this class is completely determined by the equivalence class of its associated noncommutative vector bundle. This work lays the groundwork of the noncommutative hermitian geometry, which investigates noncommutative analogues of complex manifolds, vector bundles, and hermitian metrics by drawing on ideas from both complex hermitian geometry and operator theory. We also examine noncommutative reproducing kernel Hilbert space models and introduce the noncommutative Gleason problem, showing that elements of the noncommutative Cowen-Douglas class are essentially (up to unitary equivalence) adjoints of left multiplication operators by noncommuting independent variables in a noncommutative reproducing kernel Hilbert space.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 47B13, 46E22, 46L52 Secondary: 46L07, 32L10

1.
Introduction

A central problem in the study of operators or tuples of operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is the identification of unitary invariants. Finding a complete set of invariants that are both comprehensive and computationally feasible is a challenging task, with only a few successful instances in practice. One significant contribution in this direction was made by Cowen and Douglas, who developed a framework for a broad class of non-normal operators or tuples of commuting operators on
\mathcal{H}
– known as the Cowen-Douglas class (
[
17
,
18
]
). However, this theory does not extend to tuples of non-commuting operators.

The purpose of this article is to initiate a systematic exploration of tuples of noncommuting operators – reminiscent of the tuple formed by the adjoints of right creation operators on the
\ell^{2}
space over a free monoid with finitely many letters – and their associated families of joint matricial eigenspaces by adapting the classical Cowen-Douglas theory to the modern context of free noncommutative (nc for short) function theory. This endeavor lays the foundation for a more general paradigm applicable to a broad class of such operator tuples, which we refer to as the nc Cowen-Douglas class. Our approach seeks to provide a comprehensive framework for analyzing tuples of bounded linear operators (not necessarily commuting) while deepening our understanding of nc function theory on the nc joint spectrum associated with such operator tuples.

We begin with the classical example: The backward shift operator
S
on the Hilbert space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
of square integrable complex sequences. Note that each
\lambda
in the open unit disc
{\mathbb{D}}
is an eigenvalue of
S
of multiplicity
1
with a canonical choice of eigenvector
(1,\lambda,\lambda^{2},\ldots)\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{N})
. Further, the operator
S-\lambda I_{\mathcal{H}}
is surjective for every
\lambda\in{\mathbb{D}}
. Generalizing the properties of backward shift, Cowen and Douglas, in their seminal paper
[
17
]
(and its follow-up
[
18
]
), introduced a class of (tuples of commuting) operators on a Hilbert space. More precisely, an
m
-tuple
T=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{m})
of commuting operators on
\mathcal{H}
is in the Cowen-Douglas class
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\mathcal{D})
over a bounded domain
\mathcal{D}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
of rank
r
if every
z\in\mathcal{D}
is a joint eigenvalue of
T
of multiplicity
r
(that is,
\dim\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\ker(T_{i}-z_{i}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})=r
), the linear span of these joint eigenspaces
\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\ker(T_{i}-z_{i}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
and the associated tuples
(T_{1}-z_{1},\ldots,T_{m}-z_{m})
have closed range in
\mathcal{H}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}
(
m
-times) for all
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\mathcal{D}
.

The key insight of Cowen and Douglas is that such a tuple
T
naturally defines a hermitian holomorphic vector bundle
E_{T}
over an open subset
\mathcal{D}_{0}
of
\mathcal{D}
– the fibre of
E_{T}
over a point
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}
is the joint eigenspace
\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\ker(T_{i}-z_{i}\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}})
. One of their striking results establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the unitary equivalence classes of these tuples
T
and the (local) equivalence classes of the associated vector bundles. Through this correspondence, the invariants of these vector bundles, like the curvature, second fundamental form and others, serve as unitary invariants for the tuple
T
. A complete set of invariants in this case are described in
[
19
]
. However, identifying a complete and computationally accessible set of invariants remains a formidable challenge except for the bundles whose fibres are
1
dimensional.

Crucial in any study of such a class is the problem of finding a canonical model of a generic element in the class. In
[
20
]
, Curto and Salinas obtained a canonical model for a tuple in
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\mathcal{D})
as the adjoint of the
m
-tuple of multiplication operators
(M_{z_{1}}^{*},\ldots,M_{z_{m}}^{*})
by the coordinate functions on a reproducing kernel Hilbert space of
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
valued holomorphic functions on the conjugated domain
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{*}=\{z:\overline{z}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}\}
. They further identified sufficient conditions on a reproducing kernel
K
defined on
\mathcal{D}^{*}
ensuring that the tuple
(M_{z_{1}}^{*},\ldots,M_{z_{m}}^{*})
on the reproducing kernel Hilbert space with the reproducing kernel
K
belongs to
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\mathcal{D})
.

We now provide a brief overview of free noncommutative function theory and refer interested readers to Section
2
for an elaborate discussion and the monograph
[
36
]
for a comprehensive treatment. One way to understand this theory is as an extension of the theory of holomorphic functions of several commuting complex variables
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})
to the study of functions of matrix tuples
Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d})
of freely noncommuting
n\times n
complex matrices
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In this framework, the set of all matrices of any size over a vector space (or more generally, over any module over a commutative ring) serves as the analogue of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
in commutative function theory – we refer to this set as the nc space over the vector space. A nc set is a subset of this nc space, which is closed under direct sum (cf. Subsection
2.1
), and a nc function is defined as a graded function on a nc set that takes values in a nc space and respects both direct sum and similarities. It turns out that any function defined on a nc set that is graded and respects intertwinings qualifies as a nc function (cf. Subsection
2.2
). When a nc space is equipped with an appropriate topology, there is a well defined notion of nc differentiability for nc functions (cf. Subsection
2.3
). All nc spaces in this article are equipped with the uniformly-open topology (cf. Subsection
2.1.3
; this topology is named as “fat topology” in
[
2
]
) – open sets in this topology are referred to as uniformly open sets – and, any nc analytic function in this topology is called the uniformly analytic nc function (cf. Definition
2.5
).

In
[
56
]
, Taylor introduced the theory of nc functions as a component of his broader framework for functional calculus, designed to apply to tuples of noncommuting operators (see also
[
57
]
). This theory was later expanded and referred to as the “Theory of Fully Matricial Functions” in the work of Voiculescu and his collaborators, particularly in the context of free probability and random matrix theory (cf.
[
58
]
, and also
[
59
,
60
]
). The “Fully Matricial Sets” in this theory correspond to nc sets in our setting, with the additional requirement that they are closed under similarities at all levels. This theory has been applied in the research of Helton, Klep, McCullough, and their collaborators, specially on topics such as Linear-Matrix Inequalities in the free noncommutative set-up (see
[
27
,
29
,
31
,
32
]
) and in noncommutative convexity (
[
34
,
35
]
) as well as noncommutative real semialgebraic geometry (
[
28
,
30
,
33
]
).

Rational functions and formal power series in
d
noncommuting indeterminates naturally emerge in the theory of automata and formal languages, as well as in the study of recognizable formal power series
[
15
,
22
,
37
,
38
,
54
,
55
]
, and as transfer functions in multidimensional systems evolving along the free monoid
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
13
,
14
]
. Further relevant work includes the contributions by Hadwin
[
25
]
and Hadwin-Kaonga-Mathes
[
26
]
, Popescu
[
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
]
on general noncommuting tuples of operators, as well as the work by Muhly and Solel
[
39
,
40
,
41
,
42
]
on generalized Hardy algebras associated with a
W^{*}
-correspondence.

In the monograph
[
36
]
, Kaliuzhnyi-Verbovetskyi and the second author of this article have developed the theory of noncommutative functions, which round off Taylor’s theory, keeping an eye on further study of noncommutative function theory. In particular, it is shown how the “invariant under direct sum and similarities” combined with some mild additional assumptions – namely, the local boundedness – lead to Taylor-Taylor series developments (at least locally) for a general noncommutative function. The recent work of Agler-McCarthy and collaborators
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
have made further progress in the theory of noncommutative functions.

As mentioned earlier, the goal of the present article is to study the tuples of noncommuting operators possessing a “nc open set” of joint eigenvalues on which the dimension of the corresponding joint eigenspaces becomes a “nc constant function”. It turns out that the family of joint nc eigenspaces of such a tuple exhibits a structure analogous to that of a hermitian holomorphic vector bundle in the classical setting. This investigation thus leads to a plausible notion of “nc hermitian holomorphic vector bundles” which could be of independent interest for further research.

Our starting point is the noncommuting tuple
(R_{1}^{*},\ldots,R_{d}^{*})
of adjoint of the right creation operators on the
\ell^{2}
space
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
over the free monoid
\mathcal{G}^{d}
(unital semi-group) of all words in
d
letters with the empty word as the unit element (cf. Example
5.3
). For every
d
tuple of
n\times n
matrices
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})
with
\left\|{X_{1}X_{1}^{*}+\cdots+X_{d}X_{d}^{*}}\right\|<1
(matrix norm), the following identity holds:

\dim\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{d}\ker\left(R_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})}\otimes R_{X_{j}}\right)\right)=n^{2},

for all sizes
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
R_{X}:{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
is the right multiplication operator. Furthermore, each of these joint kernels is a free left module over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
of rank
1
. Modeled after this example, we identify a class of noncommuting tuples of operators, referred to as the nc Cowen-Douglas class, denoted by
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
, where
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
is a uniformly open nc domain. For
\boldsymbol{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
with
T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
for some complex separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we set

D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}:=(T_{1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes R_{W_{1}},\ldots,T_{d}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes R_{W_{d}}),

for
W=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{d})\in\Omega\cap({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{m\times m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. In this definition of nc Cowen-Douglas operator, the family
\{\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}:W\in\Omega\}
of matricial eigenspaces is required to satisfy a suitable density property. Additionally,
\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
must have complex dimension
mr^{2}
as vector subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}
, for
W\in\Omega\cap({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{m\times m}
, and the range of
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
needs to be closed in
\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}
(
d
times) for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
(see Definition
5.1
).

After establishing a plausible definition of the nc Cowen-Douglas class over a uniformly open set
\Omega
, a systematic study of such operator tuples requires examining the nature of the subspace-valued mapping
W\mapsto\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
on
\Omega
. In particular, analogous to the classical case, we look for a uniformly analytic nc function
\gamma:\Omega_{0}\to\mathcal{H}_{nc}
on possibly a smaller open subset
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
, satisfying
\gamma(W)\in\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
. In other words, one is required to look for a uniformly analytic function
\gamma:\Omega_{0}\to\mathcal{H}_{nc}
such that
\mathcal{T}(W)(\gamma(W))=0
for
W\in\Omega_{0}
, where
\mathcal{T}:\Omega\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})_{nc}
is a uniformly analytic nc function given by
\mathcal{T}(W):=D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
.

To address this, it is necessary to establish the local existence of a uniformly analytic nc function
\mathcal{G}
around a given point
Y\in\Omega
that takes values in the nc space over an operator space
\mathcal{Y}
and satisfies the equation

\mathcal{T}(X)\star\mathcal{G}(X)=\mathcal{F}(X),

for given uniformly analytic nc functions
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
on
\Omega
taking values in the nc space over operator spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
, respectively, equipped with a multiplication
\star:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
, provided that
\mathcal{G}
is known at some initial point.

This result, Theorem
3.4
, constitutes one of the main contributions of this article and may be of independent interest (cf. Remark
3.5
). The first half of Section
3
is devoted to proving this result, and in the latter half discusses various special cases. Notably, it is shown that some of the technical hypotheses on
\mathcal{T}
in Theorem
3.4
are automatically satisfied in specific settings (see Theorem
3.8
). For instance, when
\mathcal{X}
is replaced by the space
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
of all bounded linear operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, equipped with the natural operator space structure, and
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{H}
are equipped with either the row, or column operator space structure, those hypotheses are inherently met.

In Section
4
, we begin by briefly revisiting the notion of the completely positive (cp) nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(K)
of uniformly analytic nc functions on a uniformly open nc domain
\Omega
(cf.
[
10
]
, see also
[
13
,
45
]
). A key result in this section is the computation of the nc difference-differential of the kernel elements, which are shown to remain kernel elements within the same space. We derive a formula for reproducing the nc difference-differential of any function in this Hilbert space.

The novel contributions of this section, however, lie in the study of the nc space over
\mathcal{H}(K)
, as explored in Subsection
4.2
. Within this nc space, we identify certain special matrices whose entries are kernel elements in
\mathcal{H}(K)
, referred to as the
generalized kernel elements
(cf. Section
4.2
). Using the generalized kernel elements and their nc different-differentials, respectively, we establish identities for reproducing both the functional values and the values of the nc difference-differential of any
F\in\mathcal{H}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Additionally, the action of the adjoint of left multiplication operators by the nc coordinate functions on nc difference-differential of the
generalized kernel elements
has been computed (see Proposition
4.10
). This computation is then utilized to describe the structure of certain joint invariant subspaces of the tuple formed by the adjoints of left multiplication operators by the nc coordinate functions on the given nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space (see Corollary
4.11
).

An important observation made along the way is that each level of this nc space,
\mathcal{H}(K)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, admits the canonical Hilbert
C^{*}
module structure over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
induced from the Hilbert space structure on
\mathcal{H}(K)
. Leveraging this Hilbert
C^{*}
module structure on
\mathcal{H}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
, we derive several equivalent criteria for two important properties of the kernel
K
: (1) Non-degeneracy:
\ker K(W,W)\cap\{P\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}:P\geq 0\}=\{0\}
and (2) Strict positivity:
K(W,W)(P)>0
whenever
P>0
, for
W\in\Omega\cap({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{m\times m}
. These results are formalized in Theorem
4.15
and in Theorem
4.18
, respectively. This analysis, we believe, may be of independent interest, offering insights into the intrinsic geometry of the reproducing kernel Hilbert space with a cp nc kernel. Later in Section
6
, we make use of the results in this section to construct and study the model space of a nc Cowen-Douglas operator tuple and the associated local operators.

The contents of Section
5
constitute the nucleus of the present article. We begin this section by introducing the nc Cowen-Douglas class
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
of noncommuting tuples of operators (see Definition
5.1
). As mentioned earlier, for
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
, the kernel of
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
is required to be of complex dimension
mr^{2}
as vector subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}
(
d
times) for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
. However, with the help of Flanders’ theorem (see
[
23
, Theorem 1]
), we prove that the kernel of
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
is, in fact, a free left submodule of
\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}
of free rank
r
over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
. This key observation plays a significant role in understanding such operator tuples and, alongside other properties of the family
\{\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}:W\in\Omega\}
, sheds light on what a plausible notion of vector bundles in the free noncommutative category would be.

Based on these ideas, we lay the foundation for nc hermitian holomorphic vector bundles in the latter half of this section. We first define what a trivial nc holomorphic vector bundle is and then restrict out attention to those nc vector bundles which arise as holomorphic subbundles of a trivial nc vector bundle (see Definition
5.9
and
5.10
). The nc sections, bundle homomorphisms and transition data of nc vector bundles are explained in Subsections
5.2.1
and
5.2.2
. Along the way, we establish the nc analogue of (Čech) cocycle conditions, which closely resemble their classical counterparts. Notably, we show that nc vector bundles over certain nc domains, whose nc cocycles are cohomologous to the trivial nc cocycle, are isomorphic to trivial nc vector bundles.

With the foundation of nc holomorphic vector bundles in place, we proceed to define a hermitian structure for these bundles (see Definition
5.18
). Specifically, a nc trivial bundle with the nc space over a Hilbert space as its fibre naturally inherits a canonical hermitian structure. This structure arises from the matrix sesquilinear form associated with Pisier’s OH matrix norms (see
[
44
, Chapter 7, pg. 122]
,
[
21
, Proposition 3.5.2]
for OH matrix norm). Subbundles of this nc trivial bundle are then equipped with the hermitian structures induced from the one defined on the ambient bundle.

It is worth noting that nc functions on a nc domain do not generally form a sheaf which is a crucial obstruction to carry over the classical notion of vector bundles in the realm of nc function theory. However, we believe that these embedded subbundles represent an important first step in understanding the structure of general nc vector bundles and their associated differential geometric properties – a direction we plan to explore in a follow-up article. Moreover, this restricted class of nc vector bundles aligns well with the objectives of this article, as demonstrated later in this subsection. Specifically, we show that any operator tuple in
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
gives rise to such a vector bundle over a uniformly open nc subdomain of
\Omega
with the help of Theorem
3.4
. Finally, we conclude this subsection by establishing a relationship between the unitary invariants of an element in
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
and those of the associated vector bundle (see Theorem
5.26
).

Following the work of Curto and Salinas in
[
20
]
on the construction of the models for classical Cowen-Douglas tuples, we develop a model for an element in the nc Cowen-Douglas class
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
in Section
6.1
. It is shown that such a tuple can be realized as the adjoint of the
d
-tuple of left multiplication operators by the nc coordinate functions on a nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space. This Hilbert space consists of uniformly analytic nc functions on some nc uniformly open subset of
\Omega^{*}
(
\Omega^{*}:=\{W:W^{*}\in\Omega\}
), taking values in the nc space over
\mathcal{L}({\mathbb{C}},{\mathbb{C}}^{r})\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{r}
. Furthermore, we note that these nc reproducing kernels determine the
d
-tuples of operators in
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
up to unitary equivalence.

In Subsection
6.2
, we generalize the notion of local operators, initially introduced for commuting operator tuples in the classical Cowen-Douglas class (
[
17
, §1.5, pp. 190]
), to the noncommutative setting. We define a tuple of nc local operators of order
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
in Equation (
6.8
), denoted by
N^{(\ell)}_{\boldsymbol{T},W}
, associated to an element
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
at the point
W\in\Omega
. Informally, these tuples of operators are the restriction of the tuple
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
to its “generalized eigenspace at
W
in nc setting”. Using the Taylor-Taylor series of uniformly analytic nc functions, we show that if the tuples of nc local operators of all orders associated to
\boldsymbol{T}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}
in
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
are unitarily equivalent, then so is
\boldsymbol{T}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}
. It would be interesting to investigate if the unitary equivalence of
N^{(\ell)}_{\boldsymbol{T},W}
and
N^{(\ell)}_{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}},W}
up to finite order would be sufficient for unitary equivalence for
\boldsymbol{T}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}
. We plan to pursue this project in the sequel.

After developing a model for elements in the nc Cowen-Douglas class, a natural question arises: Under what conditions is the tuple of adjoints of the left multiplication operators on a cp nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space of uniformly analytic nc functions itself in the nc Cowen-Douglas class? In addressing this question, we introduce the notion of the “noncommutative Gleason problem”, which we explore in Section
7.1
.

The classical Gleason problem was first studied by Andrew Gleason in
[
24
]
, where he examined the maximal ideals of a commutative Banach algebra. In particular, he proved that if the maximal ideal of functions vanishing at the origin in the Banach algebra
\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{B}}(0,1))
is finitely generated, then it must be generated by the coordinate functions. Here,
\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{B}}(0,1))
denotes the Banach algebra of holomorphic functions on the open unit ball
{\mathbb{B}}(0,1)
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
that extend continuously to the boundary equipped with the supremum norm. The question of whether the coordinate functions generate the maximal ideals in an algebra of holomorphic functions is now known as the
Gleason problem
.

In the noncommutative setting, we show that the nc Gleason problem (see Equation (
7.2
)) is always globally solvable when the domain is a uniformly open nc set that is right admissible (see Theorem
7.3
). This is in contrast to the classical case, where solutions are not always guaranteed globally. Moreover, the solution to the nc Gleason problem is uniquely determined by the given function (see Theorem
7.1
), unlike the classical case, where uniqueness holds only for planar domains.

Finally, in Section
7.2
, we show that any nc Cowen-Douglas tuple can be realized as the tuple of adjoint of the left multiplication operators on a cp nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space of uniformly analytic nc functions where the nc Gleason problem is solvable (see Theorem
7.8
).

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we review some foundational concepts from noncommutative function theory, which will serve as a basis for the discussions in subsequent sections. Our presentation largely follows the comprehensive treatment provided in
[
36
]
, a definitive resource on the subject of noncommutative functions, to which we refer interested readers for a more detailed discussion and proofs.

First, let us fix some notations. For a set
\mathcal{S}
and
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
\mathcal{S}^{n\times m}
as the set of all
n\times m
matrices with entries in
\mathcal{S}
. When
\mathcal{S}={\mathbb{C}}
, we use the usual notation
\mathrm{GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
for the set of all
n\times n
invertible matrices. As mentioned earlier, the shorthand notation “nc” is used for the word “noncommutative” in what follows.

2.1.
Noncommutative domains

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a commutative ring with identity. For a module
\mathcal{M}
over
\mathcal{R}
, we define the
nc space over

\mathcal{M}

( 2.1 )

\mathcal{M}_{nc}:=\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}^{n\times n}.

A subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{M}
is called a
nc set
if it is closed under the direct sum: For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Omega_{n}\oplus\Omega_{m}:=\left\{A\oplus B:=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{smallmatrix}\right]:A\in\Omega_{n},~B\in\Omega_{m}\right\}

where
\Omega_{j}=\Omega\cap\mathcal{M}^{j\times j}
,
j=n,m
. Evidently, for any
X\in\Omega_{s}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the
ms\times ms
matrix
X^{\oplus m}:=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}X&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&X\end{smallmatrix}\right]
is in
\Omega
. We call this matrix
X^{\oplus m}
as the
m
-th amplification of
X
.

Observe that the matrices over
\mathcal{R}
act from the left and the right on the matrices over
\mathcal{M}
by the usual matrix multiplication obtained from the action of
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{M}
. Namely, for
A\in\mathcal{M}^{n\times m}
,
\mathsf{X}\in\mathcal{R}^{p\times n}
and
\mathsf{Y}\in\mathcal{R}^{m\times q}
, the matrix
\mathsf{X}A\mathsf{Y}
makes sense as an element in
\mathcal{M}^{p\times q}
. In the case of
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{R}^{d}
, we identify the elements in
\mathcal{M}^{n\times m}
as
d
-tuples of
n\times m
matrices over
\mathcal{M}
. Under this identification, the direct sum of these tuples and the action of the matrices over
\mathcal{R}
on
\mathcal{M}^{n\times m}
happen to be entry-wise of these tuples.

While nc sets are the natural domains for defining nc functions, an additional condition is required to develop a robust difference-differential calculus for these functions. Specifically, a nc set must be closed under the formation of upper (or lower) triangular block matrices with arbitrary upper (or lower, respectively) blocks. However, this condition is too restrictive to hold in general—it fails even for fundamental examples such as the nc ball and the nc poly-disc. The appropriate notion, as it turns out, is more subtle and is introduced as follows.

A nc set
\Omega\subset\mathcal{M}_{nc}
is said to be
right admissible
(
left admissible
) if for every
A\in\Omega_{n}
,
B\in\Omega_{m}
and
C\in\mathcal{M}^{n\times m}
(
C\in\mathcal{M}^{m\times n}
, respectively) there exists an invertible element
\mathsf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&\mathsf{r}C\\
0&B\end{smallmatrix}\right]\in\Omega_{n+m}
(
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\
\mathsf{r}C&B\end{smallmatrix}\right]\in\Omega_{n+m}
, respectively).

2.1.1.
Full noncommutative sets.

Later, on several occasions, we will need additional structure on the nc sets to study the nc functions defined on them. For our purpose, it is customary to consider the nc space
\mathcal{V}_{nc}
over a complex vector space
\mathcal{V}
.

Following
[
11
, Definition 2.4]
recall that a nc set
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
is said to be
left-full
if
\Omega
is
invariant under left injective intertwining
: For
n\geq m
,
A\in\Omega_{n}
and
X\in\mathcal{V}^{m\times m}
, if there exists an injective
\mathsf{I}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}
such that
A\mathsf{I}=\mathsf{I}X
, then
X\in\Omega_{m}
.

Analogously, we say that
\Omega
is
right-full
if it is
invariant under right surjective intertwining
: For
n\geq m
,
A\in\Omega_{n}
and
X\in\mathcal{V}^{m\times m}
, if there exists an surjective
\mathsf{J}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times n}
such that
\mathsf{J}A=X\mathsf{J}
, then
X\in\Omega_{m}
.

So we define a nc set
\Omega
is
bi-full
if it is invariant under both the
left injective intertwining
as well as the
right surjective intertwining
.

Notice that any left or right full nc set is invariant under the similarity. Further, a similar argument as in
[
11
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2, pp 15]
shows that a nc set
\Omega
is
bi-full
if and only if it is
closed under the restriction to both invariant and co-invariant subspaces
: For
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
A\in\Omega_{n+m}
, whenever there exists an invertible
\mathsf{Q}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{(n+m)\times(n+m)}
such that
\mathsf{Q}A\mathsf{Q}^{-1}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}X&Z\\
0&Y\end{smallmatrix}\right]
with
X\in\mathcal{V}^{n\times n},~Y\in\mathcal{V}^{m\times m},~Z\in\mathcal{V}^{n\times m}
, then not only is
\mathsf{Q}A\mathsf{Q}^{-1}
in
\Omega_{n+m}
, but also
X\in\Omega_{n}
and
Y\in\Omega_{m}
.

2.1.2.
Operator spaces and complete boundedness

To perform analysis on a nc set, it is essential to fix a topology on these sets. This is achieved by equipping the nc space
\mathcal{V}_{nc}
over a vector space
\mathcal{V}
with a topology induced by an operator space structure on
\mathcal{V}
. Before proceeding, we first recall the definition of an operator space, following
[
21
, Chapter 2]
; see also
[
43
,
44
]
for additional references.

A normed linear space
\mathcal{V}
is said to be an
operator space
if it is equipped with a family of norms
\|\cdot\|_{n}
on
\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and there is a linear map
\varphi:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})
for some Hilbert space
\mathcal{X}
, such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the map

( 2.2 )

\varphi^{(n)}:=\varphi\otimes\mbox{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}:\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{X})^{n\times n}~~~\mbox{ defined by }~~~(\!(v_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{n}\mapsto(\!(\varphi(v_{ij}))\!)_{i,j=1}^{n}

is a linear isometry. Such a space can be represented as a system of norms
\{\|\cdot\|_{n}\}
on
\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying the following two conditions:

(
R1
)

\|A\oplus B\|_{n+m}=\mbox{max}\{\|A\|_{n},\|B\|_{m}\}
,
A\in\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
,
B\in\mathcal{V}^{m\times m}
;

(
R2
)

\|\mathsf{X}A\mathsf{Y}\|_{m}\leq\|\mathsf{X}\|~\|A\|_{n}~\|\mathsf{Y}\|
,
A\in\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
,
\mathsf{X}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times n}
,
\mathsf{Y}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}
,

as proved by Ruan (see
[
21
, Proposition 2.3.6]
).

Let
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
be operator spaces and
T:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
be a linear mapping. Then
T
is said to be
completely bounded
if

\left\|{T}\right\|_{\text{cb}}:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|{T\otimes\mbox{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}}\right\|<\infty,

and
completely contractive
if
\left\|{T}\right\|_{\text{cb}}\leq 1
where
\left\|{T\otimes\mbox{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}}\right\|
is the norm of the operator
T\otimes\mbox{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}:\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n\times n}
.

2.1.3.
Uniformly-open topology

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an operator space and
s\in\mathbb{N}
. For
X\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
r>0
, we define a
nc ball
centred at the point
X
with radius
r
as

B_{nc}(X,r):=\coprod_{m=1}^{\infty}B(X^{\oplus m},r)=\coprod_{m=1}^{\infty}\left\{A\in\mathcal{V}^{ms\times ms}:\left\|A-X^{\oplus m}\right\|<r\right\}.

It can be seen (see
[
36
, Proposition 7.12]
) that the
nc balls
of the above kind form a basis for a topology on
\mathcal{V}_{nc}
. This topology is known as the
uniformly-open topology
. We call the open sets in this topology as
uniformly open
. The uniformly-open topology is never Hausdorff ( cf.
[
36
, Remark 7.13]
). However, the multiplication by a scalar as a mapping
{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathcal{V}_{nc}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{nc}
is continuous in the uniformly-open topology on
\mathcal{V}_{nc}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be an operators space and the nc space
\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be equipped with the uniformly-open topology. Then a nc set
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
is said to be a nc domain if it is uniformly open and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Omega_{n}\subset\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
is connected.

2.2.
Noncommutative functions

Let
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
be operator spaces and
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a nc set. A map
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
is said to be a
nc function
if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

f
is graded:
f(\Omega_{n})\subset\mathcal{W}^{n\times n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(2)

f
respects direct sums:
f(X\oplus Y)=f(X)\oplus f(Y)
for all
X,Y\in\Omega
;

(3)

f
respects similarities:
f(\mathsf{A}X\mathsf{A}^{-1})=\mathsf{A}f(X)\mathsf{A}^{-1}
for all
X\in\Omega_{n}
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mbox{GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
such that
\mathsf{A}X\mathsf{A}^{-1}\in\Omega_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

It is well known (see
[
36
, Proposition 2.1]
) that the conditions
(2)
and
(3)
in the definition above can be replaced by a single condition, namely, a graded mapping
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
respects the direct sums and similarities if and only if it obeys the
intertwining
: For
X\in\Omega_{n}
,
Y\in\Omega_{m}
and
\mathsf{A}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}
,

( 2.3 )

f(X)\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}f(Y)\quad\text{whenever}\quad X\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}Y.

J. Taylor (cf.
[
57
]
) used this condition to define a nc function. A nc function of the above kind is called a
nc function of order
0
. For a more detailed study of nc functions and their various applications in other fields of mathematics, we refer the readers to
[
2
,
36
,
58
]
.

2.2.1.
Examples

(a) A prototypical example of nc functions is a nc polynomial. In particular, note that the nc polynomial
p:{\mathbb{C}}^{2}_{nc}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{nc}
of degree
6
defined by

p(X_{1},X_{2})=\mathrm{Id}+5X_{1}+8X_{1}X_{2}+11X_{2}X_{1}+7X_{1}^{3}X_{2}^{2}+3X_{2}X_{1}^{2}X_{2}X_{1}+2X_{2}^{6}

is a nc function where
\mathrm{Id}
denotes the identity matrix of appropriate size. For instance, if
X_{1},X_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
then
Id
is the
n\times n
identity matrix.

(b) Let
T:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
be a linear mapping of operator spaces
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
. Then note that
T
gives rise to a nc function
\widetilde{T}:\mathcal{V}_{nc}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
such that

T_{n}:=\widetilde{T}|_{\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}}:=\widetilde{T}\left((\!(v_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{n}\right)=(\!(T(v_{ij}))\!)_{i,j=1}^{n}

is the linear mapping from
\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
to
\mathcal{W}^{n\times n}
, that is,
T_{n}=T\otimes\mbox{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}
.

2.2.2.
Higher order nc functions

There is a notion of higher order nc functions, which are vaguely mappings from the cartesian product of nc sets to a certain space of multi-linear mappings with the property that on each variable, such a mapping becomes a nc function in the sense mentioned above. For instance, if
f:\Omega^{0}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{nc}
and
g:\Omega^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{nc}
are nc functions of order
0
on nc sets
\Omega^{0}
and
\Omega^{1}
, respectively, then the function
F:\Omega^{0}\times\Omega^{1}\rightarrow\coprod_{m,n\geq 1}\mathcal{L}({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m})
defined by

F(Z,W):=f(Z)\otimes g(W):{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}

is a nc function of order
1
in the sense of the definition given in Section 3.1 in
[
36
]
(also, cf.
[
36
, Remark 3.6]
). For
k+1
vector spaces
\mathcal{W}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{W}_{k}
, we denote
\mathcal{T}^{k}(\Omega,\mathcal{W}_{0,nc},\ldots,\mathcal{W}_{k,nc})
as the space of all
k
-th order nc functions
f
on
\Omega\times\cdots\times\Omega
(
k+1
times) with

f(\Omega_{n_{0}},\ldots,\Omega_{n_{k}})\subset\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{W}_{1}^{n_{0}\times n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{W}_{k}^{n_{k-1}\times n_{k}},\mathcal{W}_{0}^{n_{0}\times n_{k}}).

For a detailed discussion on higher order nc functions, we refer the readers to
[
36
, Chapter 3]
. However, the definition of nc functions of order
1
will be given at the beginning of Section
4
in the context of nc reproducing kernels.

2.3.
Noncommutative analyticity

The objective of the present subsection is to introduce noncommutative analytic functions with respect to the uniformly-open topology.

2.3.1.
Noncommutative difference-differential operators

Let
\Omega
be a right admissible nc set and
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
be a nc function. Then
\ell
-th order right nc difference-differential operator
is defined as the linear map

\Delta_{R}^{\ell}:\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Omega,\mathcal{W}_{nc})\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\ell}(\Omega,\mathcal{W}_{nc},\underbrace{\mathcal{V}_{nc},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{nc}}_{\ell-\mbox{times}})

where for
f\in\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Omega,\mathcal{W}_{nc})
and for every
X^{0}\in\Omega_{n_{0}},\ldots,X^{\ell}\in\Omega_{n_{\ell}}
,
Z^{1}\in\mathcal{V}^{n_{0}\times n_{1}},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{n_{\ell-1}\times n_{\ell}}
such that

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}X^{0}&Z^{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&X^{1}&Z^{2}&\ddots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
\vdots&&\ddots&X^{\ell-1}&Z^{\ell}\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&0&X^{\ell}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]\in\Omega_{n_{0}+n_{1}+\cdots+n_{\ell}},

\Delta_{R}^{\ell}f
is given by the formula:

( 2.4 )

f\left(\left[\begin{smallmatrix}X^{0}&Z^{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&X^{1}&Z^{2}&\ddots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
\vdots&&\ddots&X^{\ell-1}&Z^{\ell}\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&0&X^{\ell}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right)=\\
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}f(X^{0})&\Delta^{1}_{R}f(X^{0},X^{1})(Z^{1})&\cdots&\cdots&\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(X^{0},\ldots,X^{\ell})(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
0&f(X^{1})&\Delta^{1}_{R}f(X^{1},X^{2})(Z^{2})&\cdots&\Delta^{\ell-1}_{R}f(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})(Z^{2},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&f(X^{\ell-1})&\Delta^{1}_{R}f(X^{\ell-1},X^{\ell})(Z^{\ell})\\
0&\cdots&\cdots&0&f(X^{\ell})\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right].

The block structure in this equation is preserved due to
f
being nc (cf.
[
36
, Theorem 3.11]
). When
X^{0}=X^{1}=\cdots=X^{\ell}=Y\in\Omega_{s}
, we get
\ell
-th order right nc differential operator
at
Y
, which is the
\ell
-linear mapping

( 2.5 )

\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(Y,\ldots,Y):(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{s\times s}

defined by Equation (
2.4
). In a similar manner, one can define the
left nc difference-differential operator

\Delta_{L}f
by evaluating
f
on a lower triangular bi-diagonal block matrix. It follows that the left and right nc difference-differential operators are related via the following identity (see
[
36
, Proposition 2.8]
)

( 2.6 )

\displaystyle\Delta^{\ell}_{L}f(X^{0},X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell-1},X^{\ell})(Z^{1},Z^{2},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1},Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(X^{\ell},X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell-1},X^{0})(Z^{\ell},Z^{2},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1},Z^{1}).

When
\mathcal{V}={\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, we define, for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
X\in\Omega_{n}
,
Y\in\Omega_{m}
, the
j
-th right partial difference-differential operator

\Delta_{R,j}f(X,Y):{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n\times m}
by

( 2.7 )

\Delta_{R,j}f(X,Y)(Z)=\Delta_{R}f(X,Y)(Z\varepsilon_{j})

where
\{\varepsilon_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
is the standard ordered basis for
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
. By linearity, we then have

( 2.8 )

\Delta_{R}f(X,Y)(Z)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Delta_{R,j}f(X,Y)(Z_{j}).

Furthermore,
\Delta_{R}
satisfies the following nc finite difference formula: For
X
and
Y
as above and for
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times n}
,

( 2.9 )

\mathsf{S}f(X)-f(Y)\mathsf{S}=\Delta_{R}f(Y,X)(\mathsf{S}X-Y\mathsf{S})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\Delta_{R,j}f(Y,X)(\mathsf{S}X_{j}-Y_{j}\mathsf{S}).

Now using the nc finite difference formula for the higher order nc functions (see
[
36
, Theorem 3.19, pp 54]
), one obtains the following Taylor-Taylor formula (see
[
36
, Theorem 4.2, pp 62]
).

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{T}^{0}(\Omega,\mathcal{W}_{nc})
and
Y\in\Omega_{s}
. Then for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
and arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
X\in\Omega_{ms}
,

( 2.10 )

f(X)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N}\sum_{|\beta|=\ell}\big(X-Y^{\oplus m}\big)^{\odot_{s}\beta}\Delta^{\beta^{\top}}_{R}f(Y,\ldots,Y)\\
+\sum_{|\beta|=N+1}\big(X-Y^{\oplus m}\big)^{\odot_{s}\beta}\Delta^{\beta^{\top}}_{R}f(\underbrace{Y,\ldots,Y}_{N+1~\text{times}},X).

Note that Equations (
2.8
), (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) are held by
\Delta_{L}
as well.

2.3.2.
Amplifying operators

An
\ell
-linear map
T:\mathcal{V}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
can be viewed as a linear transformation
T:\mathcal{V}^{\otimes\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
. For every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, this map can be extended to a linear mapping
(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes\ell})^{n\times m}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n\times m}
by applying
T
entry-wise. Composing this extension with the canonical identification
(\mathcal{V}^{n\times m})^{\otimes\ell}\simeq(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes\ell})^{n\times m}
, we obtain an
\ell
-linear map
T_{n,m}:(\mathcal{V}^{n\times m})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{n\times m}
, which we refer to as the
amplification
of
T
. When
n=m
, we denote this map as
T_{n}
and call it the
n
-th amplification of
T
. For
m=\ell=1
, this reduces to the standard notion of the
n
-th amplification
of a matrix.

In the special case where
\mathcal{V}=({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{s\times s}\simeq({\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s})^{d}
, an
\ell
-linear map
T:\left(({\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s})^{d}\right)^{\ell}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
extends to an
\ell
-linear map
T_{n}:(({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{ns\times ns})^{\ell}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{ns\times ns}
as above using the identification
({\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s})^{n\times n}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{ns\times ns}
. Following
[
36
]
, this amplified map can be obtained as

T_{n}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=(Z^{1}\odot_{s}\cdots\odot_{s}Z^{\ell})T

for
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{ns\times ns})^{d}=(({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{s\times s})^{n\times n}
using the faux product
\odot_{s}
for
n\times n
matrices over the tensor algebra
\mathbb{T}(({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{s\times s})
.

The amplification of the nc differential operators acts on the amplifying points in the nc set according to the formula below (cf.
[
36
, Proposition 3.3]
):

(\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(Y^{\oplus n},\ldots,Y^{\oplus n}))(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(Y,\ldots,Y)_{n}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}).

2.3.3.
Canonical intertwining conditions

The nc differential operators of a nc function obey certain intertwining conditions due to the property mentioned in Equation (
2.3
). We are now about to discuss those conditions.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a vector space,
Y\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A sequence
\{f_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{k}
of
\ell
-linear mappings satisfies the canonical intertwining conditions of order
k
at
Y
(in short
\mbox{CIC}_{k}(Y)
) if for all
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
,

f_{1}([\mathsf{S},Y])=[\mathsf{S},f_{0}],

and

\displaystyle f_{\ell}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})=\mathsf{S}f_{\ell-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})-f_{\ell-1}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},Z^{2},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1}),

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},[\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})=f_{\ell-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j-1},Z^{j}\mathsf{S},Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})

\displaystyle-f_{\ell-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},Z^{j+2},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1}),

\displaystyle f_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1},[\mathsf{S},Y])=f_{\ell-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-2},Z^{\ell-1}\mathsf{S})-f_{\ell-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})\mathsf{S}

for all
2\leq\ell\leq k
,
1\leq j\leq\ell-2
,
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
, and

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}f_{k}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})=f_{k}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1}),

\displaystyle f_{k}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j-1},Z^{j}\mathsf{S},Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})=f_{k}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},Z^{j+2},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1}),

\displaystyle f_{k}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-2},Z^{\ell-1}\mathsf{S})=f_{k}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-1})\mathsf{S},

for all
1\leq j\leq k-1
,
\mathsf{S}\in\{\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}:\mathsf{S}Y=Y\mathsf{S}\}
.

We say that
f_{0}
satisfies
\mbox{CIC}_{0}(Y)
if
[f_{0},\mathsf{S}]=0
for all
\mathsf{S}\in\{\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}:\mathsf{S}Y=Y\mathsf{S}\}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a vector space,
Y\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A sequence
\{f_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
of
\ell
-linear mappings satisfies the canonical intertwining conditions at
Y
(in short
\mbox{CIC}(Y)
) if
\{f_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{k}
satisfies
\mbox{CIC}_{k}(Y)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

Note that a sequence
\{f_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
of
\ell
-linear mappings satisfies
\mbox{CIC}(Y)
in this sense if and only if it satisfies the conditions given in
[
36
, Reark 4.3, pp 62]
.

2.3.4.
Uniformly analytic nc functions

Let
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
be operator spaces and
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a uniformly open subset. A nc function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
is said to be
Gâteaux (G-) differentiable
if for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
the function
f|_{\Omega_{n}}
is G-differentiable, that is, for
X\in\Omega_{n}
and
Z\in\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
, the G-derivative of
f
at
X
along the direction
Z
defined by

( 2.11 )

\delta f(X)(Z)=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{f(X+tZ)-f(X)}{t}=\frac{d}{dt}f(X+tZ)|_{t=0}

exists. Also, recall that
f
is called
uniformly locally bounded
if for each
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
Y\in\Omega_{s}
, there exists
\varrho>0
(depending on
Y
) such that the nc ball
B_{nc}(Y,\varrho)\subset\Omega
and
f
is bounded on
B_{nc}(Y,\varrho)
, that is, there is
M>0
such that

\|f(X)\|_{sm}\leq M~~~\mbox{for all}~~~m\in\mathbb{N}~~~\mbox{and}~~~X\in B_{nc}(Y,\varrho)_{sm}.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
be operator spaces and
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a uniformly open subset. A nc function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
is said to be uniformly analytic if
f
is uniformly locally bounded and G-differentiable on
\Omega
.

Recall from Example (b) (
2.2.1
) above that any linear mapping
T:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
gives rise to a nc function
\widetilde{T}:\mathcal{V}_{nc}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
. It follows that
\widetilde{T}
is uniformly locally bounded if and only if
\widetilde{T}
is continuous with respect to the uniformly-open topologies if and only if
T
is completely bounded. In general, the first two equivalences happen to be true for any nc functions. Moreover, we have the following striking result relating the local boundedness with the nc analyticity of a nc function.

Theorem 2.6
.

[
36
, Corollary 7.28]

Let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a uniformly open nc set. Then a nc function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{nc}
is uniformly locally bounded if and only if
f
is continuous with respect to the uniformly-open topologies if and only if
f
is uniformly analytic.

2.3.5.
Taylor-Taylor series

Likewise, in the classical complex analysis, an equivalent condition for nc analyticity is the local existence of a power series for nc functions, which we describe below. First, following
[
36
, Section 7.4, pp. 114]
, we introduce the notion of bounded and completely bounded multi-linear mappings.

For Banach spaces
\mathcal{W}_{0},\mathcal{W}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{W}_{k}
, a
k
-linear mapping
\omega:\mathcal{W}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{W}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{0}
is called
bounded
if

( 2.12 )

\|\omega\|_{\mathcal{L}^{k}}:=\sup_{\|w^{1}\|=\cdots=\|w^{k}\|=1}\|\omega(w^{1},\ldots,w^{k})\|<\infty.

We use the notation
\mathcal{L}^{k}(\mathcal{W}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{W}_{k},\mathcal{W}_{0})
for the Banach space of bounded
k
-linear mappings.

When
\mathcal{W}_{0},\mathcal{W}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{W}_{k}
are equipped with operator space structures, we define, for
n_{0},n_{1},

\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, a
k
-linear mapping
\omega^{(n_{0},\ldots,n_{k})}:\mathcal{W}_{1}^{n_{0}\times n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{W}_{k}^{n_{k-1}\times n_{k}}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{0}^{n_{0}\times n_{k}}
as

( 2.13 )

\omega^{(n_{0},\ldots,n_{k})}(W^{1},\ldots,W^{k})=(W^{1}\odot\cdots\odot W^{k})\omega,

where
\odot
is the faux product as mentioned in (
2.3.2
) and
\omega
acts on the matrix
(W^{1}\odot\cdots\odot W^{k})\in(\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{W}_{k})^{n_{0}\times n_{k}}
from right entry-wise. The
k
-linear mapping
\omega
is called
completely bounded
if

( 2.14 )

\|\omega\|_{\mathcal{L}^{k}_{\text{cb}}}:=\sup_{n_{0},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}}\|\omega^{(n_{0},\ldots,n_{k})}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{k}}<\infty.

From now on the Banach space of such completely bounded
k
-linear mappings will be denoted by
\mathcal{L}^{k}_{\text{cb}}(\mathcal{W}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{W}_{k},\mathcal{W}_{0})
. Following
[
43
, Chapter 17]
or
[
44
, Chapter 5]
, we observe that
\|\omega\|_{\mathcal{L}^{k}_{\text{cb}}}
turns out to be the completely bounded norm of
\omega
viewed as a linear mapping
\omega:\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{W}_{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{0}
where the vector spaces
(\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{W}_{k})^{n_{0}\times n_{k}}
with
n_{0},n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
are endowed with the
Haagerup norm
,
\|\cdot\|_{\text{H},n_{0},n_{k}}
:

( 2.15 )

\|W\|_{\text{H},n_{0},n_{k}}:=\inf_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k-1}}\{\|W^{1}\|_{n_{0},n_{1}}\cdots\|W^{k}\|_{n_{k-1},n_{k}}:W=W^{1}\odot\cdots\odot W^{k}\}.

In this setting, the following result relates the uniformly locally boundedness of a nc function
f
to the convergence of the Taylor-Taylor series in the uniformly-open topologies.

Theorem 2.7
.

[
36
, Theorem 7.21 and 8.11]

Let
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
be operator spaces,
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a uniformly open subset and
Y\in\Omega_{s}
for some
s\in\mathbb{N}
. If a nc function
f:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{W}_{nc}
is uniformly locally bounded, then there exists
r>0
(depending on
Y
) such that

( 2.16 )

f(X)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(X-Y^{\oplus m})^{\odot_{s}\ell}\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(Y,\ldots,Y)

for all
X\in B_{nc}(Y,r)_{ms}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where the series in the equation above converges absolutely and uniformly and
\limsup_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt[\ell]{\|\Delta^{\ell}_{R}f(Y,\ldots,Y)}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\text{cb}}}<\infty
.

Conversely, let
\{f_{\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{\infty}
be a sequence of multi-linear mappings satisfying the canonical intertwining conditions
\mbox{CIC}(Y)
and

\mu_{\text{cb}}:=\limsup_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt[\ell]{\|f_{\ell}\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\text{cb}}}}<\infty.

Then the Taylor-Taylor series

( 2.17 )

\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(X-Y^{\oplus m})^{\odot_{s}\ell}f_{\ell}

converges absolutely and uniformly for all
X\in B_{nc}(Y,\varrho)
for
0<\varrho<\frac{1}{\mu_{\text{cb}}}
, and the function defined by the formula given in (
2.17
) is an uniformly locally bounded nc function on
X\in B_{nc}(Y,\varrho)
.

3.
Solving a parametrized family of noncommuting equations

Let
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
be operator spaces,
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a uniformly open bounded nc set. Consider a bilinear mapping
\mathfrak{m}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
denoted by

\mathfrak{m}(f,g):=f\star g.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
n
-th amplification
\mathfrak{m}_{n}:\mathcal{X}^{n\times n}\times\mathcal{Y}^{n\times n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{n\times n}
of
\mathfrak{m}
as: For
F=(\!(f_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{n}
and
G=(\!(g_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{n}
,

( 3.1 )

\mathfrak{m}_{n}(F,G)_{ij}=(F\star G)_{ij}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}f_{ik}\star g_{kj},~~~1\leq i,j\leq n.

Since
\mathfrak{m}
is a bilinear mapping,
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
is invariant under the canonical left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module action on
\mathcal{X}
as well as right
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module action on
\mathcal{Y}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{m}_{n}
satisfies

\mathfrak{m}_{n}(F\mathsf{A},G)=\mathfrak{m}_{n}(F,\mathsf{A}G),~~\mathsf{A}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}.

Given operator spaces
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
equipped with such a multiplication
\star
and uniformly analytic nc functions
\mathcal{T}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{nc}
and
\mathcal{F}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{nc}
, the objective of the present section is to solve a noncommutative equation of the form

( 3.2 )

(\mathcal{T}\star\mathcal{G})(X):=\mathcal{T}(X)\star\mathcal{G}(X)=\mathcal{F}(X)

on a possibly a smaller uniformly open subset of
\Omega
for a uniformly analytic nc function
\mathcal{G}
, provided that the initial data at some point is known (cf. Theorem
3.4
). In the first half of this section, we prove the existence of a solution to the equation above in the general set-up, while in the latter half, we discuss its applications in various special cases.

3.1.
General case

We now proceed to prove one of the main results, Theorem
3.4
. Heuristically, the idea behind the proof is to construct a family of
\ell
-linear mappings
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
for
\ell\geq 0
with a prescribed growth condition on their completely bounded norm satisfying the canonical intertwining conditions CIC
(Y)
at
Y
as well as an algebraic identity obtained from Equation (
3.2
) so that the associated Taylor-Taylor series at
Y
converges uniformly on a nc ball around
Y
yielding the required uniformly analytic nc function
\mathcal{G}
. Before going into the details, we wish to recall some terminologies from algebra and present an algebraic lemma – which might be of independent interest – demonstrating the relation between the Taylor-Taylor coefficients of
\mathcal{T},\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{F}
induced from their canonical intertwining conditions together with Equation (
3.2
).

First, we introduce some useful algebraic notations. Let
\mathcal{V}
be an operator space,
Y\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
,
\mathfrak{c}:{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
be the linear mapping defined by
\mathfrak{c}(\mathsf{S}):=[\mathsf{S},Y]
and denote
\ker\mathfrak{c}=\mathcal{C}(Y)
,
\mbox{ran }\mathfrak{c}=\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Since
\mathsf{C}_{1}[\mathsf{S},Y]\mathsf{C}_{2}=[\mathsf{C}_{1}\mathsf{S}\mathsf{C}_{2},Y]
for any
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{C}_{1},\mathsf{C}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(Y)
,
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is a sub-bi-module in
\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
over
\mathcal{C}(Y)
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the subspaces
\mathcal{C}_{k}\subset(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes k}
are defined as

\mathcal{C}_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes i}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes k-1-i}

For
Y,Z^{1},\ldots,

Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
,
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
, and a family of multi-linear mappings
\{\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{1}^{s\times s}:

\ell\geq 0\}
for some operator space
\mathcal{V}_{1}
, we denote

\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell,0}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=\mathcal{G}_{\ell+1}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\mathsf{S}\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell,j}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=\mathcal{G}_{\ell+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},[\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j-1},Z^{j}\mathsf{S},\\
Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},Z^{j+2},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell,\ell}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=\mathcal{G}_{\ell+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell},[\mathsf{S},Y])-\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\mathsf{S})+\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\mathsf{S}

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
be operator spaces and
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a uniformly open nc set. Let
\mathfrak{m}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
be a bilinear mapping:
(f,g)\mapsto f\star g
. Fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
Y\in\Omega_{s}
and
H\in\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
. Consider uniformly analytic nc functions
\mathcal{T}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{nc}
and
\mathcal{F}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{nc}
satisfying
\mathcal{T}(Y)\star H=\mathcal{F}(Y)
. Assume that
\mathcal{G}_{0}=H
and
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
are multi-linear mappings for
\ell\geq 1
.

(i)

If the family of multi-linear mappings
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
,
\ell\geq 0
, satisfy the canonical intertwining conditions, then for each
k\geq 0
, they satisfy the equation

( 3.3 )

\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}\star\mathcal{G}_{j}

on
\mathcal{C}_{k}
where
\{\mathcal{T}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
and
\{\mathcal{F}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
are Taylor-Taylor coefficients of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
, respectively, at
Y
.

(ii)

If the identity given in part (i) holds on
\mathcal{C}_{k}
for
k\geq 0
, then

[\mathsf{S},\mathcal{G}_{0}]-\mathcal{G}_{1}([\mathsf{S},Y]),~\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell,0}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\in\ker\big(\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}_{0},\cdot)\big),

for
\ell\geq 1
,
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
. Here,
\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}_{0},\cdot)
is the linear mapping
\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}_{0},\cdot):\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
. Furthermore, for
\ell\geq 1
,
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
,

( 3.4 )

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell,j}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\mathcal{T}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j})\star\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell-j,0}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\mathrm{CIC}_{i,j}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=0,~\text{and}

( 3.5 )

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathrm{CIC}_{\ell,\ell}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\mathcal{T}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\star\mathcal{G}_{1}([\mathsf{S},Y])-[\mathsf{S},\mathcal{G}_{0}])

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\mathrm{CIC}_{i+1,i+1}(\mathcal{G},Y)(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})=0.

Proof.

(i) Since each of
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}
,
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
is multi-linear mapping on
(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}
, it is enough to prove that the desired identity holds on
(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{i}\times\mathcal{C}_{1}\times(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{k-1-i}
individually, for every
i=0,\ldots,k-1
. We prove this with the help of mathematical induction on
k
. Observe from the hypothesis that the identity (
3.3
) holds for
k=0
. So we assume that the identity (
3.3
) holds true for all
0\leq j\leq k-1
.

For
i=0
and
([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\times(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{k-1}
, using the canonical intertwining conditions for each of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
\mathcal{T}_{k-j}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,k
, note that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})+\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathcal{G}_{k}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left[\mathsf{S}\mathcal{T}_{k-j-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})-\mathcal{T}_{k-j-1}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\right]\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\left[\mathsf{S}\mathcal{G}_{k-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})-\mathcal{G}_{k-1}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-2}\left[\mathsf{S}\mathcal{T}_{k-j-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})-\mathcal{T}_{k-j-1}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\right]\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+(\mathsf{S}\mathcal{T}_{0}-\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathsf{S})\star\mathcal{G}_{k-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})+\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathsf{S}\mathcal{G}_{k-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt-\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathcal{G}_{k-1}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left[\mathsf{S}\mathcal{T}_{k-1-j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1-j})-\mathcal{T}_{k-1-j}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\right]\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{S}\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})-\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-1}).

A similar computation also yields that the desired identity holds on the summand
(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{k-1}\times\mathcal{C}_{1}
for
i=k-1
. Finally, for
1\leq i\leq k-2
, and
(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},[\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,

Z^{k-1})\in(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{i}\times\mathcal{C}_{1}\times(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{k-1-i}
, using the canonical intertwining conditions for each of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
\mathcal{T}_{k-j}
and
\mathcal{G}_{j}
for
j=0,\ldots,k
, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},[\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-i-2}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},[\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\mathcal{T}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i})\star\mathcal{G}_{k-i}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\mathcal{T}_{i+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},[\mathsf{S},Y])\star\mathcal{G}_{k-i-1}(Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-i-2}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},[S,Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\mathcal{T}_{i+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i}S)\star\mathcal{G}_{k-i-1}(Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})\!-\!\mathcal{T}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i})\star\mathcal{G}_{k-i}(SZ^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{j=k-i+1}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j})\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j+1},\ldots,Z^{i},[S,Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k-i-2}\mathcal{T}_{k-j-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i}S,Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt-\sum_{j=0}^{k-i-2}\mathcal{T}_{k-j-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},SZ^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-j-1})\star\mathcal{G}_{j}(Z^{k-j},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\mathcal{T}_{i+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i}S)\star\mathcal{G}_{k-i-1}(Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})\!-\!\mathcal{T}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i})\star\mathcal{G}_{k-i}(SZ^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt+\sum_{j=k-i+1}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j})\star\mathcal{G}_{j-1}(Z^{k-j+1},\ldots,Z^{i}S,Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt-\sum_{j=k-i+1}^{k}\mathcal{T}_{k-j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{k-j})\star\mathcal{G}_{j-1}(Z^{k-j+1},\ldots,Z^{i},SZ^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i}S,Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})-\mathcal{F}_{k-1}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},SZ^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{k}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{i},[S,Y],Z^{i+1},\ldots,Z^{k-1}).

(ii) We begin by pointing out that the given identity turns out to be
\mathcal{F}_{1}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{G}_{0}+\mathcal{T}_{0}\mathcal{G}_{1}
, and consequently,

\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathcal{G}_{1}([\mathsf{S},Y])=\mathcal{F}_{1}([\mathsf{S},Y])-\mathcal{T}_{1}([\mathsf{S},Y])\star\mathcal{G}_{0}=[\mathsf{S},\mathcal{F}_{0}]-[\mathsf{S},\mathcal{T}_{0}]\star\mathcal{G}_{0}=\mathcal{T}_{0}\star[\mathsf{S},\mathcal{G}_{0}]

where the last equality holds since
\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathcal{G}_{0}=\mathcal{F}_{0}
. Now for
\ell\geq 1
,
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
, note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathcal{G}_{\ell+1}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{\ell+1}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1-i}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{S}\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt-\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1-i}([\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{S}\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}[\mathsf{S}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt-\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})]\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{S}\bigg(\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.45377pt-\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\sum_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\!+\!\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathsf{S}\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathsf{S}\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(\mathsf{S}Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})

verifying that
\mbox{CIC}_{\ell,0}(\mathcal{G})
is in the kernel of
\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}_{0},.)
.

Next, we show that the identity (
3.4
) holds for any
\ell\geq 1
,
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
. For
\ell\geq 1
,
Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
, we use the notation

(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell}):=(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},[\mathsf{S},Y],Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}),

and compute

\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\mathcal{G}_{\ell+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})=\bigg(\mathcal{F}_{\ell+1}-\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}\bigg)(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
=\mathcal{F}_{\ell+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
=\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell}\!\!\!\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j}\mathsf{S},Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\!\!\!\sum_{i=\ell-j}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
+\mathcal{T}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j})\star\mathsf{S}\mathcal{G}_{\ell-j}(Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\!\!\!\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell}\!\!\!\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
=-\mathcal{T}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j})\star\text{CIC}_{\ell-j,0}(Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\!\!\!\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j}\mathsf{S},Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
-\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=\ell-j+2}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell+1-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
=-\mathcal{T}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j})\star\text{CIC}_{\ell-j,0}(Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})+\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell}\!\!\!\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j}\mathsf{S},Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
-\!\!\!\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell-1}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}\star\mathcal{G}_{i+1}(Z^{1},\ldots,[\mathsf{S},Y],\ldots,Z^{\ell})\\
=\mathcal{T}_{0}\star\left[\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j}\mathsf{S},Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\mathcal{G}_{\ell}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{j},\mathsf{S}Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})\right]-\mathcal{T}_{j}(Z^{1},\ldots,\\
Z^{j})\star\text{CIC}_{\ell-j,0}(Z^{j+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell})-\sum_{i=\ell-j+1}^{\ell-j}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-i}(Z^{1},\ldots,Z^{\ell-i})\star\text{CIC}_{i,j}(Z^{\ell-i+1},\ldots,Z^{\ell}),

verifying Equation (
3.4
). Finally, an analogous computation as above for
\ell\geq 1
,
Z^{1},\ldots,

Z^{\ell}\in\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
ensures the validation of Equation (
3.5
).
∎

We now present the main theorem of this section for which we need the following technical definition. Also, note that the notations and the terminologies used in the following theorem have been introduced in the beginning of this subsection.

Definition 3.2
.

A closed subspace
\mathcal{S}
of an operator space
\mathcal{V}
is said to be completely complemented if there exists a closed subspace
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{V}
and a completely bounded projection
Q:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}
onto
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
with
\ker Q=\mathcal{S}
.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that if
\mathcal{V}
is a Hilbert space equipped with the row operator space structure then every closed subspace of it is completely complemented as shown in Lemma
3.6
. A similar proof in the same lemma can be used to show that so is true for any column operator Hilbert spaces as well.

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
be operator spaces and
\mathfrak{m}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}
be a completely contractive bilinear mapping:
(f,g)\mapsto f\star g
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathcal{V}_{nc}
be a bounded, uniformly open nc set which is right admissible and fix
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
Y\in\Omega_{s}
so that
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is a complemented sub-bi-module of
\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
over
\mathcal{C}(Y)
. Consider uniformly analytic nc functions

\mathcal{T}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{nc}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{F}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{nc}

satisfying
\mathcal{F}(X)\in\mathrm{ran}(\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(X),.))
for
X\in\Omega
and
\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(Y),H)=\mathcal{F}(Y)
for some
H\in\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(Y),.):\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{s\times s}
satisfies the following conditions:

(a)

\ker\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(Y),.)
is a completely complemented subspace of
\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
with the associated projection
Q:\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
to be a
\mathcal{C}(Y)
bi-module homomorphism, and

(b)

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\{X_{n}\}\subset\Omega_{ms}
,
X_{n}
converges to
Y^{\oplus m}
,
K_{n}\in\text{ran }(\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(X_{n}),.))
and
K_{n}
converges to
K
, then
K\in\text{ran }(\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(Y^{\oplus m}),.))
(in particular,
\mathfrak{m}(\mathcal{T}(Y)^{\oplus m},.)
has closed range for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
).

Then there exist a uniformly open neighbourhood
U
of
Y
and a uniformly analytic nc function
\mathcal{G}:U\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{nc}
such that

\mathcal{G}(Y)=H\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{T}(X)\star\mathcal{G}(X)=\mathcal{F}(X)~\quad\text{for all}~~X\in U.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}
be a closed sub-bi-module of
\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
over
\mathcal{C}(Y)
complementary to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
in
\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
, and
\varrho_{1}:\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\varrho^{\prime}_{1}:\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}
be the projections so that
I_{\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}}=\varrho_{1}+\varrho^{\prime}_{1}
where
I_{\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}}
is the identity operator on
\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
. Since the dimension of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is finite,
\varrho_{1}
is completely bounded and hence so is
\varrho_{1}^{\prime}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}:=(\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime})^{\otimes k}
and observe that
\mathcal{C}_{k}
is complementary to the subspace

\mathcal{C}_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes i}\otimes{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\otimes(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes k-1-i}

in
(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes k}
. Evidently,
\mathcal{C}_{k}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{k}
are also
\mathcal{C}(Y)
- bi-modules as well as
I_{(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\otimes k}}=\varrho_{k}+\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
where
\varrho_{k}
and
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
are the projections corresponding to the complementary subspaces
\mathcal{C}_{k}
and
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{\prime}
, respectively. Then
\|\varrho_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\text{cb}}\leq\|\varrho_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\text{cb}}^{k}
, and consequently,
\|\varrho_{k}\|_{\text{cb}}\leq 1+\|\varrho_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\text{cb}}^{k}
. Also, note that the sub-bi-modules
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{1}^{\prime}
give rise to the completely bounded right inverse
\varphi:\mathcal{C}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
to the linear mapping
\mathfrak{c}:{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{s\times s}
defined by
\mathsf{S}\mapsto[\mathsf{S},Y]
.

Recall that both
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
, being uniformly analytic nc functions, are locally uniformly bounded. Let
r>0
be such that both
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{F}
are uniformly bounded on
{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}(1+2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}})r)
and for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
X\in{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}(1+2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}})r)
,

\mathcal{T}(X)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(X-Y^{\oplus m})^{\odot_{s}\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}(X)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(X-Y^{\oplus m})^{\odot_{s}\ell}\mathcal{F}_{\ell}

where
\mathcal{T}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{s\times s}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}^{s\times s}
are
\ell
-linear mappings satisfying the canonical intertwining conditions. Denote

\tilde{r}=2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}(1+2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}})r\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\hat{r}}=2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}(1+2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}})\hat{r},

where
\hat{r}
is defined as
0<\hat{r}\leq\text{min}\bigg\{r,\frac{1}{2\left\|{\varrho_{1}^{\prime}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}\big(1+2\left\|{\varrho^{\prime}_{1}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}\big)}\bigg\}
. Note that
\hat{r}\leq\tilde{\hat{r}}\leq\tilde{r}~\text{and}~\tilde{\hat{r}}\leq 1
since being a projection,
\left\|{\varrho^{\prime}_{1}}\right\|_{\text{cb}}\geq 1
. Let

M=\text{max}\big\{1,\left\|{\varphi\circ\varrho_{1}}\right\|_{\text{cb}},\sup\{\left\|{\mathcal{T}(X)}\right\|_{ms,\mathcal{X}},\left\|{\mathcal{F}(X)}\right\|_{ms,\mathcal{Z}}:X\in{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,\hat{r})_{m},m\in\mathbb{N}\}\big\}.

Then it follows that

( 3.6 )

\left\|{\mathcal{T}_{\ell}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\text{cb}}}\leq\frac{M}{\tilde{\hat{r}}^{\ell}}\leq\frac{M}{\hat{r}^{\ell}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|{\mathcal{F}_{\ell}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\text{cb}}}\leq\frac{M}{\tilde{\hat{r}}^{\ell}}\leq\frac{M}{\hat{r}^{\ell}},\quad\ell\geq 0.

Now finding a uniformly analytic nc function
\mathcal{G}
on some uniformly open nc neighbourhood of
Y
taking values in
\mathcal{Y}_{nc}
amounts to find
\ell
-linear mappings
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
, for
\ell\geq 0
, satisfying the canonical intertwining conditions and a positive real number
0<r^{\prime}\leq r
such that

\left\|{\mathcal{G}_{\ell}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{\text{cb}}}\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{r^{\prime\ell}},\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}

for some
M^{\prime}\geq 0
so that the Taylor-Taylor series

( 3.7 )

\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}(X-Y^{\oplus m})^{\odot_{s}\ell}\mathcal{G}_{\ell}

converges uniformly for all
X\in{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,r^{\prime})_{ms}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and the uniformly analytic nc function
\mathcal{G}
associated to the Taylor-Taylor series in (
3.7
) satisfies the identity

( 3.8 )

\mathcal{T}(X)\star\mathcal{G}(X)=\mathcal{F}(X),~X\in{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,r^{\prime}).

For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows from the conditions (a) and (b) in the hypothesis that there exists
C>0
such that

( 3.9 )

\left\|{\mathfrak{m}\left(\mathcal{T}(Y)^{\oplus m},Q_{n}(G)\right)}\right\|_{ms,\mathcal{Z}}\geq C\left\|{Q_{m}(G)}\right\|_{ms,\mathcal{Y}}

where
G=(\!(G_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}\in\mathcal{Y}^{ms\times ms}
with
G_{ij}\in\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
for
i,j=1,\ldots,m
. Set
\alpha=\text{min}\{1,C\}
,

r^{\prime}=\frac{\hat{r}\alpha}{2M}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{r^{\prime}}=\frac{\tilde{\hat{r}}\alpha}{2M}.

Then note that
r^{\prime}\leq\hat{r}\leq\tilde{\hat{r}}
,
r^{\prime}\leq\tilde{r}^{\prime}
and
{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,r^{\prime})\subset{\mathbb{B}}_{nc}(Y,\hat{r})
.

We now construct the
\ell
-linear mappings
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
satisfying the canonical intertwining conditions as well as Equation (
3.8
), or equivalently, for each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,

( 3.10 )

\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\mathcal{T}_{\ell-j}\star\mathcal{G}_{j}=\mathcal{F}_{\ell}

and
\left\|{\mathcal{G}_{\ell}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{cb}}\leq r^{\prime-{\ell}}\mathrm{max}\big\{1,\left\|{H}\right\|_{s,\mathcal{Y}}\big\}
with the help of mathematical induction on
\ell
. Note that
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathcal{T}(Y)
.

Assume that we have defined the desired
\ell
-linear mappings
\mathcal{G}_{\ell}
satisfying Equation (
3.10
) and

\left\|{\mathcal{G}_{\ell}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{\ell}_{cb}}\leq\tilde{r}^{\prime-\ell}\mathrm{max}\big\{1,\left\|{H}\right\|_{s,\mathcal{Y}}\big\}\leq r^{\prime-\ell}\mathrm{max}\big\{1,\left\|{H}\right\|_{s,\mathcal{Y}}\big\},

for
\ell=0,1,\ldots,k-1
so that the canonical intertwining conditions hold up to order
k-1
(see Definition
2.3
). We then explicitly construct a
k
-linear mapping
\mathcal{G}_{k}:(\mathcal{V}^{s\times s})^{k}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}^{s\times s}
with the prescribed growth on it’s complete norm –
\left\|{\mathcal{G}_{k}}\right\|_{\mathcal{L}^{k}_{cb}}\leq r^{\prime-k}\mathrm{max}\big\{1,\left\|{H}\right\|_{s,\mathcal{Y}}\big\}
– satisfying (
3.10
) and the canonical intertwining conditions up to order
k
.
