\displaystyle\left\langle f\otimes\mathsf{S},\left(M_{Z_{k}}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{m\times m}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\otimes R_{W_{k}^{*}}\right)\left(\mathtt{A}(_{1^{*}}\Delta_{R}^{\ell}K(\cdot,W,\ldots,W)\xi)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})\mathsf{B}\right)\right\rangle_{HS}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\!\!\left(M_{Z_{k}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{m\times m}\!-\!\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\otimes R_{W_{k}}\right)\!(f\otimes\mathsf{S}),\!\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\!K_{\!{}_{(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})}W^{\oplus{\ell}},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i})\mathtt{A}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\!\right\rangle_{HS}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle M_{Z_{k}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{m\times m}(f\otimes\mathsf{S}),K_{{}_{(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})}W^{\oplus{\ell}},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i})\mathtt{A}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\right\rangle_{HS}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\otimes R_{W_{j}}(f\otimes\mathsf{S}),K_{{}_{(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})}W^{\oplus{\ell}},\left[\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i})\mathtt{A}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\end{smallmatrix}\right]}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\right\rangle_{HS}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle{}_{(X_{k}^{1},\ldots,X_{k}^{\ell})}W_{k}^{\oplus{\ell}}f\left({}_{(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})}W^{\oplus{\ell}}\right)\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{\ell m}}\langle\mathsf{S},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle f\left({}_{(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})}W^{\oplus{\ell}}\right)\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right],\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\end{smallmatrix}\right]\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{{\ell}m}}\langle\mathsf{S}W_{k},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle X^{\ell}_{k}\Delta_{L}^{{\ell}-1}f(W,\ldots W)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{{\ell}-1})(\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{m}}\langle\mathsf{S},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle W_{k}\Delta_{L}^{\ell}f(W,\ldots,W)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})(\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{m}}\langle\mathsf{S},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle\Delta_{L}^{\ell}f(W,\ldots,W)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})(\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{m}}\langle\mathsf{S}W_{k},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left\langle f\otimes\mathsf{S},\mathtt{A}(_{1^{*}}\Delta_{R}^{{\ell}-1}K(\cdot,W,\ldots,W)\xi)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{{\ell}-1})(X^{\ell}_{k})^{*}\mathsf{B}\right\rangle_{HS}

\displaystyle+\left\langle f\otimes\mathsf{S},\mathtt{A}(_{1^{*}}\Delta_{R}^{{\ell}}K(\cdot,W,\ldots,W)\xi)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})[W_{k}^{*},\mathsf{B}]\right\rangle_{HS}

where the last equality holds due to the following identities

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle W_{k}\Delta_{L}^{\ell}f(W,\ldots,W)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})(\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{m}}\langle\mathsf{S},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\left[\left\langle\Delta_{L}^{\ell}f(W,\ldots,W)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})(\mathtt{A}^{*}(\mathsf{e}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*})),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\xi\right\rangle_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{m}}\langle\mathsf{S}W_{k},\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{B}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{r}\overline{\xi}_{t}\left\langle\left(\Delta_{L}^{\ell}f_{t}(W,\ldots,W)(X^{1},\ldots,X^{\ell})\otimes\mathsf{S}\right)(\mathtt{A}^{*}),[W_{k}^{*},\mathsf{B}]\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}.

Since
\mathcal{H}_{m}
is the dense linear span of elementary tensors of the form
f\otimes\mathsf{S}
with
f\in\mathcal{H}(K)
and
\mathsf{S}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
, the proof of part (ii) follows from the computation above.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
be a uniformly open set and
\mathcal{H}(K)
be a nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space with the cp nc kernel
K:\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathcal{L}({\mathbb{C}}_{nc},{\mathbb{C}}^{r\times r}_{nc})
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
, let
\mathfrak{L}_{\ell}
and
\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}
, respectively, denote the left module and bi-module generated by the set
\{K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s},

(_{1*}\Delta_{R}^{j}K(\cdot,W,\ldots,W)\sigma_{t})(X^{1},\ldots,X^{j}):1\leq j\leq{\ell},1\leq s,t\leq r\}
over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
. Then

(i)

\mathfrak{L}_{\ell}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{d}\left(M^{*}_{Z_{j}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\otimes R_{W^{*}_{j}}\right)^{-1}(\mathfrak{B}_{{\ell}-1})

where
\mathfrak{B}_{0}
is the bi-module generated by the set
\{K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}:1\leq s\leq r\}
.

(ii)

For each
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}
is an invariant subspace of
M^{*}_{Z_{j}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\otimes R_{W^{*}_{j}}
,
1\leq j\leq d
.

(iii)

\overline{\bigvee_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}\left\{h_{ij}:(\!(h_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}\right\}}=\mathcal{H}(K)
.

Proof.

(i) First, we observe from Equation (
4.31
) that

(M^{*}_{Z_{j}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\otimes R_{W^{*}_{j}})(\mathfrak{L}_{\ell})\subset\mathfrak{B}_{\ell-1},

for every
1\leq j\leq d
. Also, note that

\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\left(\mathfrak{L}_{\ell}\right)=rm^{2}\frac{(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}{2}~\text{and}~\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\left(\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}\right)=rm^{2}\frac{\ell(\ell+3)}{2}.

Since the dimension of the joint kernel of the operators
M^{*}_{Z_{j}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\otimes R_{W^{*}_{j}}
,
1\leq j\leq d
– which is
\ker D_{\boldsymbol{M}^{*}-W^{*}}
– is
rm^{2}
as a vector space over
{\mathbb{C}}
, it follows that

\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\ell})=\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\left(\bigcap_{j=1}^{d}\left(M^{*}_{Z_{j}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\otimes R_{W^{*}_{j}}\right)^{-1}(\mathfrak{B}_{\ell-1})\right)

verifying the desired equality.

(ii) The proof follows from part (i) and Equation (
4.31
).

(iii) Let
f\in\mathcal{H}(K)
be perpendicular to the subspace
\overline{\bigvee_{l\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}}\left\{h_{ij}:(\!(h_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}\in\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}\right\}}
. Then
F:=f\otimes I_{m}
is perpendicular to
\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and consequently, it follows from Equation (
4.30
) that
F
is identically zero on a uniformly-open neighbourhood of
W
. Thus
f
is identically zero on a uniformly-open neighbourhood of
W
verifying that
f=0
in
\mathcal{H}(K)
.
∎

Remark 4.12
.

Note that the subspaces
\mathfrak{B}_{\ell}
, for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, can be viewed as matricial or nc analogues of the generalized eigenspaces of the tuple

\big(M_{Z_{1}}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}},\ldots,M_{Z_{d}}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}\big)

of order
\ell
associated to the matricial eigenvalue
W^{*}=(W_{1}^{*},\ldots,W_{d}^{*})\in\Omega^{*}_{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We make use of this corollary later in subsection
6.2
to study the local operators associated to an element in the nc Cowen-Douglas class.

4.3.
Hilbert
C^{*}
module structures on
\mathcal{H}(K)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}

As in the preceding subsection, let us consider a cp nc kernel
K:\Omega\times\Omega\to\mathcal{L}({\mathbb{C}}_{nc},{\mathbb{C}}^{r\times r}_{nc})
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We now discuss canonical Hilbert
C^{*}
left and right module structures on the space
\mathcal{H}_{m}
of
m\times m
matrices over
\mathcal{H}(K)
.

We define the mappings
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\times\mathcal{H}_{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{R}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\times\mathcal{H}_{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
by

\langle F,G\rangle_{L}:=\bigg(\!\!\bigg(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle f_{i\ell},g_{j\ell}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\bigg)\!\!\bigg)_{i,j=1}^{m}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle F,G\rangle_{R}:=\bigg(\!\!\bigg(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle f_{\ell j},g_{i\ell}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\bigg)\!\!\bigg)_{i,j=1}^{m},

respectively, where
F=(\!(f_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}
and
G=(\!(g_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}
in
\mathcal{H}_{m}
. It can be seen that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{R}
equip
\mathcal{H}_{m}
a left and right Hilbert
C^{*}
module over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
, respectively. We also point out that these two Hilbert module structures give rise to the Hilbert space structure on
\mathcal{H}_{m}
introduced in Equation (
4.20
), when we take their traces. Namely, for
F=(\!(f_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}
and
G=(\!(g_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}
in
\mathcal{H}_{m}
,

\mathrm{trace}\langle F,G\rangle_{L}=\mathrm{trace}\langle F,G\rangle_{R}=\langle F,G\rangle_{HS}.

We now study left and right Hilbert
C^{*}
module structures on
\mathcal{H}_{m}
separately in the following two subsections.

4.3.1.
Left Hilbert
C^{*}
module structures on
\mathcal{H}_{m}

Define the
left generalized evaluation functional

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
as follows:

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}(F):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{A}_{j}f_{j}(W),

where
F=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}\otimes\mathsf{A}_{j}
for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{H}(K)
and
\mathsf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{A}_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
. Since elements in
\mathcal{H}(K)
are nc functions on
\Omega
taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}_{nc}
, we consider, for each
1\leq s\leq r
, the
s
-th
left generalized evaluation functional

\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{L}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}\quad\text{as}\quad\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{L}(F):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathsf{A}_{j}f_{j,s}(W),

where
F\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
as above with each
f_{j}=(f_{j,1},\ldots,f_{j,r})
, and
f_{j,s}
is a nc function on
\Omega
taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}_{nc}
, for
1\leq j\leq n
,
1\leq s\leq r
. We now study the elementary properties of these left generalized evaluation functionals.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
be as above.

(1)

Reproducing property: For
F\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
,
W\in\Omega_{m}
and
1\leq s\leq r
,

\langle F,K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\rangle_{L}=\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{L}(F).

(2)

For
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{A}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathsf{A}_{r}\end{bmatrix}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
,

(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}].
In particular, both
\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}
and
(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}
are left module maps over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
.

(3)

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}:({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
is the linear operator given by the formula:

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}):=\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\big(\!\big(\mathsf{E}(W)_{st}\big)\!\big)_{s,t=1}^{r}:=\begin{bmatrix}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}\mathsf{E}(W)_{1s}\\
\vdots\\
\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}\mathsf{E}(W)_{rs}\end{bmatrix},

where each
\mathsf{E}(W)_{st}
is an
m\times m
matrix defined as

\mathsf{E}(W)_{st}:=\big(\!\big(\operatorname{trace}\big(K_{st}(W,W)(E_{ij}^{*})\big)\big)\!\big)_{i,j=1}^{m}.

In particular, when
r=1
,
\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\mathsf{A})=\mathsf{A}K(W,W)^{*}(I_{m})^{\top}
.

Proof.

Let
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\in\mathcal{H}(K)
and
\mathsf{A}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
so that
f\otimes\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
. Then we compute, for
1\leq s\leq r
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
,

\displaystyle\langle f\otimes\mathsf{A},K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\rangle_{L}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f\otimes\mathsf{A},K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\big\rangle_{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f,K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\mathsf{A}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}=\sum_{\ell,k=1}^{m}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}a_{\ell k}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{k}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}\hskip 36.135pt

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}a_{\ell j}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}=\sum_{i,\ell=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\sum_{j=1}^{m}\overline{a_{\ell j}}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}\hskip 7.22743pt

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,\ell=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\mathsf{A}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}=\sum_{i,\ell=1}^{m}\big\langle\mathsf{A}f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}\hskip 46.97505pt

\displaystyle=\mathsf{A}f_{s}(W)=\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{L}(f\otimes\mathsf{A}).\hskip 292.69371pt

This completes the proof of part (1).

For the proof of (2), let
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
be as given and
F=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes\mathsf{B}_{i}
with
\mathsf{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{B}_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
and each
f_{i}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}f_{i,s}\otimes\sigma_{s}
. Then we compute

\langle\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}(F),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\langle\mathsf{B}_{i}f_{i,s}(W),\mathsf{A}_{s}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{B}_{i}f_{i,s}(W)\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{B}_{i}f_{i,s}(W)\big)\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}\\
=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{R}(F)\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\langle F,K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\rangle_{L}\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}=\big\langle F,\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{L},

where the second last equality holds since
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L}
defines a left Hilbert
C^{*}
module structure on
\mathcal{H}_{m}
. It thus verifies that

(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}].

Finally, for the proof of part (3), we compute, for
1\leq t\leq r
and
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
,

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}),I_{m}\otimes\sigma_{t}\big\rangle=\big\langle(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}),(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(I_{m}\otimes\sigma_{t})\big\rangle_{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\big\langle\mathsf{A}_{s}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}],I_{m}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{t}]\big\rangle_{L}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}\big\langle K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s},K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{t}\big\rangle_{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell,k=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}},K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell},\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\mathsf{A}_{s}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}},K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\mathsf{A}_{s}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}}(W)(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}\rangle\mathsf{A}_{s}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle K(W,W)(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i})(\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}\rangle\mathsf{A}_{s}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}\big[\sum_{i,\ell=1}^{m}\!\!\big(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle K(W,W)(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i})(\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}\rangle\big)\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}\big[\sum_{i,\ell=1}^{m}\text{trace}\big(K(W,W)_{ts}(\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{i})\big)\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}\big]=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}\mathsf{E}(W)_{ts}.

This validates the desired formula for
\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})
.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

For any
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
,
W\in\Omega_{m}
and
1\leq s,t\leq r
,

\langle\mathsf{A}K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s},\mathsf{B}K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{t}\rangle_{L}=\mathsf{A}\mathsf{E}(W)_{st}\mathsf{B}^{*}

where
\mathsf{E}(W)_{st}
is as defined in Proposition
4.13
.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from the proof of part (3) in the preceding proposition.
∎

We now express certain non-degeneracy criteria of the cp nc kernel
K
in terms of the left generalized evaluation functional in the theorem below.

Theorem 4.15
.

The following are equivalent for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
.

(1)

\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{1\times r}
is a surjective.

(2)

The matrix

\big(\!\big(K_{ts}(W,W)^{*}(I_{m})\big)\!\big)_{s,t=1}^{r}

is invertible, where
K_{ts}(W,W)^{*}(I_{m})
is
(s,t)
-th block,
1\leq s,t\leq r
and
K_{ts}(W,W)^{*}
is the adjoint of the linear mapping
K_{ts}(W,W):{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt product on
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
.

(3)

K(W,W):{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times r}
satisfies: For
P\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
with
P\geq 0
,

K(W,W)(P)=0\hskip 7.22743pt\text{if and only if}\hskip 7.22743ptP=0.

(4)

\mathcal{H}_{K}
has the following property:

\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}}\ker f(W)=\{0\}.

Proof.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2): This equivalence follows from part (3) of Proposition
4.13
along with the following observation: For
1\leq s,t\leq r
and
1\leq i,j\leq m
, the
(i,j)
-th entry of the matrix
\mathsf{E}(W)_{st}
is

\displaystyle\overline{\text{trace}\big(K_{st}(W,W)(\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{i})\big)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}},K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle K_{ts}(W,W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}),I_{m}\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}},

which is the
(i,j)
-th entry of the matrix
K_{ts}(W,W)^{*}(I_{m})
.

(3)
\Leftrightarrow
(4): First, we observe that

\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}}\ker f(W)=\big\{v^{*}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times 1}:K_{W,v,y}=0,~\text{for all}~y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}\big\}.

Indeed, if
K_{W,v,y}=0
for all
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}
, then

\langle f,K_{W,v,y}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{K}}=\langle f(W)(v^{*}),y\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}=0,

for all
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}
implying that
v^{*}\in\ker f(W)
. Conversely, if
v^{*}\in\ker f(W)
, the reverse to this argument shows that
K_{W,v,y}=0
for all
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}
.

Now assuming (3) and applying it for rank
1
non-negative definite matrices, it can be seen that

\big\{v^{*}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times 1}:K_{W,v,y}=0,~\text{for all}~y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}\big\}=\{0\}

which verifies (4). Conversely, if
K(W,W)(P)=0
for
P\geq 0
, writing
P=Q^{*}Q
, we notice that

\displaystyle K(W,W)(P)=K(W,W)(Q^{*}Q)

\displaystyle=K(W,W)\left(Q^{*}\big(\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}\big)Q\right)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}K(W,W)\big(Q^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}Q\big).

Consequently, for any
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}
, we have that

\langle K(W,W)(P)y,y\rangle=\langle K(W,W)(Q^{*}Q)y,y\rangle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle K(W,W)\big(Q^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}Q\big)y,y\rangle\\
=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell}Q,y},K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell}Q,y}\rangle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\left\|{K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell}Q,y}}\right\|^{2}.

Since
K(W,W)(P)=0
, it follows from the equation above that
K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell}Q,y}=0
for all
\ell=1,\ldots,m
. Now applying (4), we see that
Q=0
.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(4): Since
\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{1\times r}
is subjective, observe that its adjoint mapping
(\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W})^{*}:({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{1\times r}\to\mathcal{H}_{m}
defined by

(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}],\hskip 7.22743pt\text{for}~~\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{A}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathsf{A}_{r}\end{bmatrix}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1},

is injective. We first show that
K_{W,v,y}=0
for all
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{rm\times 1}
only if
v=0
. For
v\in{\mathbb{C}}^{1\times m}
, let
K_{W,v,y}=0
for all
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{rm\times 1}
and
\mathsf{A}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
be such that
\varepsilon_{1}\mathsf{A}=v
and
\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}=0
for
j=2,\ldots,m
. Then we consider the element
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}:=\mathsf{A}\otimes\sigma_{1}
(recall that
\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r}\}
is the standard ordered basis for
{\mathbb{C}}^{r\times 1}
) and note that

(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{L})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=\mathsf{A}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{1}]=\begin{bmatrix}K_{W,\varepsilon_{1}\mathsf{A},\varepsilon_{1}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{1}}&\cdots&K_{W,\varepsilon_{1}\mathsf{A},\varepsilon_{m}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{1}}\\
0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}=0,

since
K_{W,\varepsilon_{1}\mathsf{A},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{1}}=K_{W,v,\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{1}}
, for
j=1,\ldots,m
, and
K_{W,v,y}=0
for all
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{rm\times 1}
. Consequently, from the injectivity of
(\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W})^{*}
we see that
\mathsf{A}=0
implying that
v=0
.

Conversely, we prove that
(\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W})^{*}
is injective assuming that
\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}}\ker f(W)=\{0\}
. So let
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
and
(\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=0
. Then, for any
F=f\otimes I_{m}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
with
f=\sum_{s=1}^{r}f_{s}\otimes\sigma_{s}
, we have from the computation in the proof of part (2) in Proposition
4.13
that

\langle F,(\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})\rangle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}f_{s}(W)A_{s}^{*}=0.

In other words, the range of the adjoint of linear mapping
\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\end{bmatrix}:{\mathbb{C}}^{mr}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
is contained in the kernel of the mapping
\begin{bmatrix}f_{1}(W)&\cdots&f_{r}(W)\end{bmatrix}:{\mathbb{C}}^{mr}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
, for every
f\in\mathcal{H}(K)
. Since
\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}}\ker f(W)=\{0\}
, it follows that
A_{j}=0
for
1\leq j\leq m
. It thus concludes that
(\mathsf{EV}^{L}_{W})^{*}
is injective.
∎

4.3.2.
Right Hilbert
C^{*}
module structures on
\mathcal{H}_{m}

Define the
right generalized evaluation functional

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
as follows:

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}(F):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}(W)\mathsf{A}_{j},

where
F=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j}\otimes\mathsf{A}_{j}
for some
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}\in\mathcal{H}(K)
and
\mathsf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{A}_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
. Since elements in
\mathcal{H}(K)
are nc functions on
\Omega
taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}^{r}_{nc}
, we consider, for each
1\leq s\leq r
, the
s
-th
right generalized evaluation functional

\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{R}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
as

\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{R}(F):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}f_{j,s}(W)\mathsf{A}_{j},

where
F\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
as above with each
f_{j}=(f_{j,1},\ldots,f_{j,r})
, and
f_{j,s}
is an uniformly analytic nc function on
\Omega
taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}_{nc}
, for
1\leq j\leq n
,
1\leq s\leq r
. As in the preceding subsection, we now demonstrate some elementary properties of the right generalized evaluation functionals.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
be as above. Then

(1)

Reproducing property: For
F\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
,
W\in\Omega_{m}
and
1\leq s\leq r
,

\langle F,K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\rangle_{R}=\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{R}(F).

(2)

For
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathsf{A}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathsf{A}_{r}\end{bmatrix}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
,

(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}]\mathsf{A}_{s}.
In particular, both
\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}
and
(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}
are right module maps over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
.

(3)

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}:({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
is the linear operator given by the formula:

\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=K(W,W)(I_{m})\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\sum_{s=1}^{r}K_{1s}(W,W)(I_{m})\mathsf{A}_{s}\\
\vdots\\
\sum_{s=1}^{r}K_{rs}(W,W)(I_{m})\mathsf{A}_{s}\end{bmatrix}

Proof.

Let
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\in\mathcal{H}(K)
and
\mathsf{A}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
so that
f\otimes\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
. Then we compute, for
1\leq s\leq r
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
,

\displaystyle\langle f\otimes\mathsf{A},K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\rangle_{R}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f\otimes\mathsf{A},K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}}\otimes\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\big\rangle_{R}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f,K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}}\big\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}\mathsf{A}=\sum_{\ell,k=1}^{m}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}a_{\ell k}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{\ell}^{*}\varepsilon_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}a_{ik}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{k}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)\big(\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{ik}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\big),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)(\mathsf{A}\varepsilon_{k}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{k}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\big\langle f(W)\mathsf{A}(\varepsilon_{k}^{*}),\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}\big\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\varepsilon_{k}

\displaystyle=f_{s}(W)\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{R}(f\otimes\mathsf{A}).

This completes the proof of part (1).

For the proof of (2), let
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times 1}
be as given and
F=\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}\otimes\mathsf{B}_{i}
with
\mathsf{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{B}_{n}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
and each
f_{i}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}f_{i,s}\otimes\sigma_{s}
. Then we compute

\langle\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}(F),\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\langle f_{i,s}(W)\mathsf{B}_{i},\mathsf{A}_{s}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}f_{i,s}(W)\mathsf{B}_{i}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i,s}(W)\mathsf{B}_{i}\big)\\
=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}\mathsf{EV}_{W,s}^{R}(F)=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\mathsf{A}_{s}^{*}\langle F,K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\rangle_{R}=\big\langle F,\sum_{s=1}^{r}K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}\mathsf{A}_{s}\big\rangle_{R},

where the second last equality holds since
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{R}
defines a right Hilbert
C^{*}
module structure on
\mathcal{H}_{m}
. Consequently, we have

(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}})=\sum_{s=1}^{r}[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}]\mathsf{A}_{s}.

Finally, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
, the computation below proves the claim in part (3).

\displaystyle\big\langle\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R}\circ(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}),I_{m}\otimes\sigma_{t}\big\rangle=\big\langle(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{A}}),(\mathsf{EV}_{W}^{R})^{*}(I_{m}\otimes\sigma_{t})\big\rangle_{R}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\big\langle[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s}]\mathsf{A}_{s},[K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{t}]I_{m}\big\rangle_{R}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\big\langle K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s},K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{t}\big\rangle_{R}\mathsf{A}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell,k=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}},K_{W,\varepsilon_{\ell},\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{\ell}\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}},K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}}(W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}),\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}\rangle\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m}\langle K(W,W)(\varepsilon_{i}^{*}\varepsilon_{i})(\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}),\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}\rangle\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\langle K(W,W)(I_{m})(\varepsilon_{j}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{s}),\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{t}\rangle\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}_{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\sum_{j,k=1}^{m}\langle K(W,W)_{ts}(I_{m})\varepsilon_{j}^{*},\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\rangle\varepsilon_{k}^{*}\varepsilon_{j}\mathsf{A}_{s}=\sum_{s=1}^{r}K(W,W)_{ts}(I_{m})\mathsf{A}_{s}.

∎

Corollary 4.17
.

For any
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
,
W\in\Omega_{m}
and
1\leq s,t\leq r
,

\langle\mathsf{A}K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{s},\mathsf{B}K(\cdot,W)\sigma_{t}\rangle_{R}=K_{ts}(W,W)(\mathsf{B}^{*}\mathsf{A})

where
K_{ts}(W,W)(\mathsf{B}^{*}\mathsf{A})
is the
(t,s)
-th block of the block matrix
K(W,W)(\mathsf{B}^{*}\mathsf{A})
.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from the proof of part (3) in the preceding proposition.
∎

In the following theorem, we obtain certain strict positivity criteria of the nc cp kernel
K
in terms of the right generalized evaluation functional.

Theorem 4.18
.

The following are equivalent for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
.

(1)

\mathsf{EV}^{R}_{W}:\mathcal{H}_{m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{1\times r}
is a surjective.

(2)

K(W,W)(I_{m})\in({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times r}
is an invertible matrix.

(3)

K(W,W):{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}\to({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m})^{r\times r}
satisfies: For
P\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
,

P>0\hskip 7.22743pt\text{implies}\hskip 7.22743ptK(W,W)(P)>0.

(4)

\mathcal{H}_{K}
has the following property:

\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}}\ker f(W)^{*}=\{0\}.

Proof.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2): This equivalence follows from part (3) of Proposition
4.16
.

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3): Note that (3) trivially implies (2). So we prove
K(W,W)(P)>0
with
P>0
, assuming that
K(W,W)(I_{m})
is an invertible matrix. For
P>0
, we have that

P\geq\left\|{P^{-1}}\right\|^{-1}I_{m}>0,

since being strictly positive,
P
is invertible. Consequently,

K(W,W)(P)\geq K(W,W)\big(\left\|{P^{-1}}\right\|^{-1}I_{m}\big)=\left\|{P^{-1}}\right\|^{-1}K(W,W)(I_{m})>0.

(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(4): We first prove that the matrix
K(W,W)(I_{m})
is an invertible if and only if

\big\{y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}:K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y}=0,~1\leq j\leq m\big\}=\{0\}.

If
K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, then for any
x\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}
, we have that

\langle K(W,W)(I_{m})y,x\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y},K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},x}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}=0.

Consequently,
K(W,W)(I_{m})y=0
, implying that
y=0
since
K(W,W)(I_{m})
is invertible.

On the other hand, if
K(W,W)(I_{m})y=0
for some
y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}
, then

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\langle K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y},K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}=\langle K(W,W)(I_{m})y,y\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}=0.

But this implies that
\left\|{K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y}}\right\|=0
for all
1\leq j\leq m
. Therefore,
y=0
.

Finally, we observe the following realization

\big\{y\in{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}:K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y}=0,~1\leq j\leq m\big\}=\bigcap_{f\in\mathcal{H}_{K}}\ker f(W)^{*}.

Indeed, the condition,
K_{W,\varepsilon_{j},y}=0
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, is equivalent to the fact that

\langle f(W)(\varepsilon_{j}^{*}),y\rangle_{{\mathbb{C}}^{mr\times 1}}=\langle f,K_{W,\varepsilon,y}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}(K)}=0,

for all
1\leq j\leq m
and
f\in\mathcal{H}(K)
. In other words,
y\perp\text{range}f(W)
, for all
f\in\mathcal{H}(K)
, verifying the claim.
∎

5.
Noncommutative Cowen-Douglas class and vector bundles

In this section, we introduce a class of noncommuting tuples of bounded linear operators on a complex separable Hilbert space that exhibit properties reminiscent of those in the classical Cowen-Douglas class. We refer to this class as the noncommutative (nc) Cowen-Douglas class. We then explore various examples that illustrate and justify our definition.

Next, we define noncommutative (nc) Hermitian holomorphic vector bundles over a nc domain and, using Theorem
3.4
, establish that every tuple in the nc Cowen-Douglas class naturally gives rise to such a nc vector bundle. Furthermore, the unitary invariants of operator tuples in this class are entirely determined by the invariants of the associated nc vector bundles. This connection paves the way for a deeper study of invariants of nc vector bundle, which we wish to pursue in subsequent work.

5.1.
Noncommutative Cowen-Douglas class

We begin by fixing some notations. Recall that
\mathcal{H}
is a complex separable Hilbert space. Throughout this subsection we let
\mathcal{V}={\mathbb{C}}^{d}
endowed with an operator space structure and
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
be a nc domain in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})
is a
d
-tuple of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{d})\in\Omega_{m}
, we define the associated operator

D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}:\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}

by

( 5.1 )

D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}:=(T_{1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes R_{W_{1}},\ldots,T_{d}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes R_{W_{d}}).

Note that the kernel of
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
is nothing else but the joint kernel of the operators
T_{1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes R_{W_{1}},\ldots,T_{d}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}}-\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{H}}\otimes R_{W_{d}}
.

Definition 5.1
.

A
d
-tuple
\boldsymbol{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})
of bounded linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
is said to be in the noncommutative (nc) Cowen-Douglas class
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
of rank
r
over
\Omega
if for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{d})\in\Omega_{m}
, the operators
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
satisfy the following three conditions:

(i)

\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}(\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W})=rm^{2}
,

(ii)

\overline{\bigvee_{W\in\Omega_{m},m\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{h_{ij}:(\!(h_{ij})\!)_{i,j=1}^{m}\in\ker D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}\right\}}=\mathcal{H}
,

(iii)

range of
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
is closed in
\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathcal{H}^{m\times m}
.

Remark 5.2
.

(i) Note that unlike the classical Cowen-Douglas class over a domain in
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
, the tuples of operators in the nc Cowen-Douglas class do not have the same dimensional joint eigenspaces. However, they do have a constant dimensional kernel of the associated operator
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-W}
associated to all
W\in\Omega_{m}
for each fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
, which is essentially due to the free noncommutative phenomenon.

(ii) Also, observe that for any bounded domain
\mathcal{D}\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
, every
d
-tuple of operators in the classical Cowen-Douglas class of rank
r
over
\mathcal{D}
induces an operator tuple in the nc Cowen-Douglas class of rank
r
over the nc domain
\Omega=\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}^{\oplus n}
, where
\mathcal{D}^{\oplus n}=\{z^{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus z^{n}:z^{1},\ldots,z^{n}\in\mathcal{D}\}
and
z\oplus w
is the
d
-tuple of
2\times 2
diagonal matrix
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}z&0\\
0&w\end{smallmatrix}\right]
.

We now discuss some non-trivial examples of tuples of operators in the nc Cowen-Douglas class which naturally appear in the study of noncommutative function theory on the noncommutative balls and poly-discs. We call the unit ball in the row operator Hilbert space
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
as nc unit ball in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
which is, by definition,

{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}:=\coprod_{m=1}^{\infty}\left\{(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})\in\left({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}\right)^{d}:\sum_{i=1}^{d}X_{i}X_{i}^{*}<I_{m}\right\}.

Similarly, we define the nc unit poly-disc in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
to be the unit ball in
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
equipped with the maximum norm operator space structure –
\|(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})\|_{\infty}=\mathrm{max}\{\|X_{1}\|,\ldots,\|X_{d}\|\}
,
\|\cdot\|
is the usual matrix norm on
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
– namely,

{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{nc}:=\coprod_{m=1}^{\infty}\left\{(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})\in\left({\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}\right)^{d}:\|X_{i}\|<1,~i=1,\ldots,d\right\}.

Example 5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{d}
be the free monoid (unital semi-group) of all words in the
d
letters
\{1,\ldots,d\}
with the empty word
\emptyset
(the word containing no letters) as the unit. For a noncommuting indeterminate
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
and a word
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{G}^{d}
, set
x^{\alpha}=x_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{\alpha_{k}}
. Also, write
|\alpha|
for the length of the word
\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}
. Then we define the
noncommutative
\ell^{2}
-space

\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
over
\mathcal{G}^{d}
as

\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d}):=\bigg\{f:\mathcal{G}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}:\|f\|_{2}^{2}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}|f(\alpha)|^{2}<+\infty\bigg\}.

As in the commutative case
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
turns out to be a Hilbert space with the hermitian product

\langle f,g\rangle_{\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}f(\alpha)\overline{g(\alpha)}.

The set
\{e_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}\}
then turns out to be the canonical orthonormal basis for
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
, where
e_{\alpha}:\mathcal{G}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is defined as
e_{\alpha}(\beta)=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
. The
right creation operators

R_{1},\ldots,R_{d}
are the linear operators on
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
acting on the orthonormal basis
\{e_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}\}
as follows:

R_{j}(e_{\alpha})=e_{\alpha j},\quad 1\leq j\leq d,~~~\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}.

Note that the action of their adjoints
R_{1}^{*},\ldots,R_{d}^{*}
on
\{e_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}\}
become

R_{j}^{*}(e_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}e_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k-1}}&\mbox{if}&\alpha_{d}=j\\
0&&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,~~~1\leq j\leq d,~~~\alpha=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}\in\mathcal{G}^{d},k\in\mathbb{N}.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{d})\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{n}
, it can be seen that

\{X^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}e_{\alpha}\otimes X^{\alpha}\in\left(\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})\right)^{n\times n}.

Consequently, for each
1\leq j\leq d
, we have

R_{j}^{*}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}}\bigg(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}e_{\alpha}\otimes X^{\alpha}\bigg)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}e_{\alpha}\otimes X^{\alpha}X_{j}.

A straightforward computation further reveals that

( 5.2 )

\ker(D_{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}-X})=\bigg\{\mathsf{A}\bigg(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}e_{\alpha}\otimes X^{\alpha}\bigg):\mathsf{A}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}\bigg\},

where
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}=(R^{*}_{1},\ldots,R^{*}_{d})
. Thus, for
X\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it confirms

\dim\ker(D_{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}-X})=n^{2}.

Moreover, the operator
D_{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}-X}
has closed range for all
X\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The
d
-tuple of operators
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}=(R^{*}_{1},\ldots,R^{*}_{d})
thus satisfies all three condition of Definition
5.1
verifying that
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}\in\mathrm{B}_{1}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{nc}
.

The Hilbert space
\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
can also be viewed as Full Fock space
\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})
over
{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}
, which by definition is

\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}):=\bigg\{f:{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{nc}:f(X)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}f_{\alpha}X^{\alpha},\{f_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})\bigg\}.

The inner product on
\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})
is given by the formula: For
f,g\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})
with
f(X)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}f_{\alpha}X^{\alpha}
and
g(X)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}g_{\alpha}X^{\alpha}
on
{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}
,

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}f(\alpha)\overline{g(\alpha)}.

With this inner product,
\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})
turns out to be a nc reproducing kernel Hilbert space (cf. Theorem
4.1
) of uniformly analytic nc functions on
{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}
with the cp nc reproducing kernel

K:{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}\times{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}\rightarrow\coprod_{n,m=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m},{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m})

defined, for any
Z\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{n}
,
W\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{m}
and
\mathsf{P}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times m}
, by

K(Z,W)(P):=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}Z^{\alpha}P(W^{\alpha})^{*}.

In this set up, it is seen that the vectors
\{X^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathcal{G}^{d}}\in\ell^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{d})
defined above correspond to the
generalized kernel elements
in
\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{nc}
(cf. Section
4.2
). Moreover, the
d
-tuple
(M_{Z_{1}}^{*},\ldots,M_{Z_{d}}^{*})
of adjoint of the left multiplication operators by the nc coordinate functions on
\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{nc}
becomes the model for the tuple
\boldsymbol{R}^{*}
as described in Subsection
6.1
.

Example 5.4
.

Other important examples of noncommuting tuples of operators in the nc Cowen-Douglas class are the
d
-tuple
\boldsymbol{M}^{*}=(M_{Z_{1}}^{*},\ldots,M_{Z_{d}}^{*})
of the adjoint of left multiplication operators
M_{Z_{1}},\ldots,M_{Z_{d}}
by the nc coordinate functions on nc Hardy space over
\mathcal{B}_{{\mathbb{B}}^{d}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{{\mathbb{D}}^{d}}
(cf.
[
45
]
). In particular, it can be shown that
\boldsymbol{M}^{*}
belongs to
\mathrm{B}_{1}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}{\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc}\big)_{nc}
and
\mathrm{B}_{1}\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}{\mathbb{D}}^{d}_{nc}\big)_{nc}
, respectively (cf.
[
45
, Remark 3.10, pp 960]
).

Remark 5.5
.

From Equation (
5.2
) it is evident that the kernel of the operator
D_{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}-X}
with
X\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{n}
is not only a complex vector space of dimension
n^{2}
, but also a free left module of rank
1
over the ring
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
.

The property of being a left module over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
is not an isolated phenomenon for the kernel of
D_{\boldsymbol{R}^{*}-X}
with
X\in({\mathbb{B}}^{d}_{nc})_{n}
. Indeed, for any
d
-tuple of operators
\boldsymbol{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{d})\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
and
X\in\Omega_{n}
, the kernel of
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-X}
turns out to be free left module of rank
r
over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
as is proved below (see Corollary
5.8
). In accomplishing our goal we recall the following non-trivial result in the study of spaces of matrices of low rank.

Theorem 5.6

(Flanders’ theorem)
.

[
23
, Theorem 1]

Let
n,p,r\in\mathbb{N}
. If all matrices in a subspace of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times p}
have rank less than or equal to
r
then the subspace has dimension less than or equal to
nr
.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
n,r\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathcal{H}
be a complex separable Hilbert space and
\mathcal{S}
be a left submodule of the module
\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
. Assume that
\mathcal{S}
has dimension
n^{2}r
over
{\mathbb{C}}
as a vector subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
. Then
\mathcal{S}
is a free left submodule of
\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
of rank
r
.

Proof.

We provide two proofs of this proposition, beginning with the first:

Consider the case when
r=1
. Suppose, for every
H\in\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
, that there exists a nonzero matrix
\mathsf{C}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
(depending on
H
) such that

\mathsf{C}H=0.

Since the entries of all the matrices in
\mathcal{S}
lie in a finite dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}
, we can pick up a basis thereof
h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}
and identify
\mathcal{S}
with a subspace of
({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})^{N}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times nN}
(where
(\mathsf{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{A}_{N})
corresponds to
\mathsf{A}_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+\mathsf{A}_{N}h_{N}
). Every matrix in this subspace necessarily has a nontrivial left kernel (given by a nonzero row of the corresponding matrix
\mathsf{C}
), implying that it’s rank is at most
n-1
. By Theorem
5.6
, the dimension of the subspace is at most
n(n-1)=n^{2}-n<n^{2}
. Consequently, there exists
H\neq 0
in
\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathsf{C}H=0
if and only if
\mathsf{C}=0
for
\mathsf{C}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
.

Note that the dimension of the subspace
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathsf{C}H:\mathsf{C}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}\}\subset\mathcal{S}
as a complex vector space is
n^{2}
. Indeed, thinking of
H
as an element in
(\mathcal{H}^{\oplus n})^{n\times 1}
as
H=(R_{1}\cdots R_{n})^{\top}
, observe that
\{B_{ij}:1\leq i,j\leq n\}
is a basis of
\mathcal{E}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
where for any
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
B_{ij}
is the matrix in
\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
whose only non-zero row is the
i
-th row which is
R_{j}
. This implies that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{E}
.

For a general
r
, we proceed as above to obtain a non-zero element
H_{1}\in\mathcal{S}
such that the subspace
\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\{\mathsf{C}H_{1}:\mathsf{C}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}\}
has complex dimension
n^{2}
. Since
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
is a semi-simple ring, so is every
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module, verifying that
\mathcal{S}
is a semi-simple left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module. Consequently, there exists a left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
submodule
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}
as left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module. But
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is then a vector subspace of
\mathcal{S}
as well and the complex dimension of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is
n^{2}r-n^{2}=n^{2}(r-1)
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is a left semi-simple module over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
, so is
\mathcal{S}/\mathcal{E}_{1}\cong\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
. Therefore, we run this process until the complex dimension of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
becomes
0
to obtain a decomposition of
\mathcal{S}
as direct sum of
r
many left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
submodules
\mathcal{E}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{E}_{r}
where
\mathcal{E}_{j}:=\{\mathsf{C}H_{j}:\mathsf{C}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}\}
for some
H_{j}\neq 0
in
\mathcal{S}
for
j=1,\ldots,r
. Finally, observe that
\{H_{1},\ldots,H_{r}\}
forms a free left module basis for
\mathcal{S}
over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
. This completes the proof.

The second proof proceeds as follows:

Any left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module is isomorphic to a direct sum of finitely many copies of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, say
\ell
many copies. Equivalently, any representation of
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
decomposes into a direct sum of copies of the standard representation. Counting the dimension of
\mathcal{S}
as a vector space over
{\mathbb{C}}
, we see that

\mathcal{S}\simeq\bigoplus_{1}^{nr}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\simeq\bigoplus_{1}^{r}\bigg(\bigoplus_{1}^{n}{{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}\bigg).

Since
\bigoplus_{1}^{n}{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
, which serves as the left regular representation and is of course a free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module of rank 1, it follows that
\mathcal{S}
is a free left module of rank
r
, completing the proof.
∎

Applying this result to a tuple of operators in
\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
and using the definition of nc Cowen-Douglas class we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.8
.

For
\boldsymbol{T}\in\mathrm{B}_{r}(\Omega)_{nc}
and
X\in\Omega_{n}
, the kernel of the operator
D_{\boldsymbol{T}-X}:\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}\rightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{n\times n})^{\oplus d}
in
\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
is a free left submodule of
\mathcal{H}^{n\times n}
of rank
r
.

5.2.
Noncommutative vector bundles

As mentioned earlier in this section, the objective of the present subsection is to introduce a concept of noncommutative hermitian holomorphic vector bundles over a nc uniformly open subset of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
. We begin with the notion of the
trivial noncommutative (nc) vector bundle
over a nc domain.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{C}}^{d}_{nc}
be a nc domain and
\mathcal{V}
be an operator space. Then a trivial noncommutative (nc) vector bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
over
\Omega
is a uniformly open subset of
({\mathbb{C}}^{d}\times\mathcal{V})_{nc}
equipped with a uniformly analytic nc surjective mapping
\pi:\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\rightarrow\Omega
such that
\pi^{-1}(Z)\simeq\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
both as a vector space as well as a free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module for all
Z\in\Omega_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Such a trivial nc vector bundle is denoted by
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
, the mapping
\pi
is called the canonical projection,
\Omega
is the base, and the set
\pi^{-1}(Z)
is called the fibre of the bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
over
Z
denoted by
\mathfrak{T}_{Z}
. The rank of a trivial nc vector bundle is defined as the dimension of
\mathcal{V}
. When
\dim\mathcal{V}=1
, we call it as trivial nc line bundle over
\Omega
and denote it by
\mathfrak{L}_{\Omega}
.
The total space of a trivial nc vector bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
is the set

\mathfrak{T}:=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}\times\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
.

For
X\in\Omega_{n}
and
Y\in\Omega_{m}
, we denote the direct sum of the fibres over
X
and
Y
as

\mathfrak{T}_{X}\oplus\mathfrak{T}_{Y}:=\left\{\bigg(\begin{bmatrix}X&0\\
0&Y\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}\bigg):A\in\mathcal{V}^{n\times n},~B\in\mathcal{V}^{m\times m}\right\},

and observe that

\mathfrak{T}_{X}\oplus\mathfrak{T}_{Y}\overset{\iota}{\hookrightarrow}\mathfrak{T}_{X\oplus Y}=\{X\oplus Y\}\times\mathcal{V}^{(n+m)\times(n+m)}.

Further, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{S}\in\text{GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
and
X\in\Omega_{n}
, whenever
\mathsf{S}X\mathsf{S}^{-1}\in\Omega_{n}
, the mapping
\mathfrak{S}:\mathfrak{T}_{X}\rightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\mathsf{S}X\mathsf{S}^{-1}}
defined by

\mathfrak{S}(X,A)=(SXS^{-1},SAS^{-1})

turns out to be an isomorphism.

We now introduce the notion of noncommutative (nc) vector bundles over

\Omega
. Given that the presheaf of nc functions on an open set is not necessarily a sheaf, we choose to define nc vector bundles as those that are embedded in a trivial nc vector bundle over
\Omega
. Moreover, we note that in the free noncommutative setting, the notions of local boundedness and analytic functions are equivalent (recall Theorem
2.6
). As a result, the only relevant notion in this context is that of holomorphic vector bundles unlike the classical case.

Definition 5.10
.

Given a trivial nc holomorphic vector bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
, let
E
be a nc subset of
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
equipped with the relative uniformly-open topology. Then we say that
E
is a noncommutative holomorphic vector bundle of rank
r
over
\Omega
if

(1)

\pi|_{E}:E\rightarrow\Omega
is surjective;

(2)

there exists an open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
of
\Omega
by uniformly open sets
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\subset\Omega
for
\alpha\in\Lambda
and uniformly analytic nc bijective function
\phi_{\alpha}:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})\rightarrow(\pi|_{E})^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})
satisfying

(2a)

\pi|_{E}\circ\phi_{\alpha}=\pi
;

(2b)

for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
Z\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
,
\phi_{\alpha}
on the fibre
\pi^{-1}\{Z\}
is linear as well as free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
module isomorphism onto its image.

We denote a nc holomorphic vector bundle by
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
. The collection
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
is called a local trivialization of the bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
.

Since each
\phi_{\alpha}
is locally uniformly bounded on
\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})
, it is evident that so is the mapping
\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}
with respect to the relative uniformly-open topology on
E
.

Remark 5.11
.

Note that the conditions (2a) and (2b) together with Proposition
5.7
imply that each fibre of a nc vector bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
of rank
r
over a point in
\Omega_{n}
is not only a complex vector space of dimension
n^{2}r
but also it is a free left module of rank
r
over the ring
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
.

We also point out that existence of local trivialization
(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})
is equivalent to the fact that there exist uniformly analytic nc functions
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}:\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{nc}
such that for every
Z\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
,
\{\gamma_{1}(Z),\ldots,\gamma_{r}(Z)\}
forms a free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{s\times s}
module basis of the fibre
(\pi|_{E})^{-1}\{Z\}
over
Z
.

5.2.1.
Sections and homomorphisms of nc vector bundles

Let
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
be a nc holomorphic vector bundle of rank
r
embedded in
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
. Then a
nc section

\sigma
of
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
is a mapping
\sigma:\Omega\rightarrow E
satisfying following properties:

(i)

\pi\circ\sigma=\mathrm{Id}_{\Omega}
where
\mathrm{Id}_{\Omega}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
is the nc identity mapping on
\Omega
;

(ii)

\sigma(X\oplus Y)=\sigma(X)\oplus\sigma(Y)
for all
X,Y\in\Omega
;

(iii)

\sigma(\mathsf{S}X\mathsf{S}^{-1})=\mathfrak{S}(\sigma(\mathsf{S}X\mathsf{S}^{-1}))=\mathsf{S}\sigma(X)\mathsf{S}^{-1}
for all
X\in\Omega_{n}
,
\mathsf{S}\in\text{GL}(n,{\mathbb{C}})
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

A nc section of
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
of the above kind is said to be uniformly analytic if it, viewed as a mapping
\sigma:\Omega\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{nc}
, is locally bounded.

A local
uniformly analytic ordered nc frame
of a nc holomorphic vector bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
is an ordered
r
-tuple
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{r}
of uniformly analytic nc sections of
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
over an uniformly open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega
such that for every
Z\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
,
\{\sigma_{1}(Z),\ldots,\sigma_{r}(Z)\}
is free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module basis for the fibre
E_{Z}:=\pi^{-1}\{Z\}
of
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
over
Z
.

Definition 5.12
.

Suppose that
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
and
E^{\prime}\overset{\pi^{\prime}}{\rightarrow}\Omega^{\prime}
are two nc holomorphic vector bundles. A nc vector bundle homomorphism from
E
to
E^{\prime}
is a uniformly analytic nc mapping
\widetilde{f}:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
— that is,
\widetilde{f}
is locally uniformly bounded with respect to the relative uniformly-open topology on
E
and
E^{\prime}
— which descends to a uniformly analytic nc map
f:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega^{\prime}
and satisfies

\pi^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{f}=f\circ\pi,

along with the property that the restriction
\widetilde{f}|_{E_{Z}}:E_{Z}\rightarrow E_{f(Z)}
is a homomorphism as complex vector spaces and left modules over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
for all
Z\in\Omega_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Further,
\widetilde{f}
is called an isomorphism of nc vector bundles if, in addition,
f^{-1}
exists and is locally uniformly bounded with respect to the relative uniformly-open topology on
E^{\prime}
and
E
, and the restriction
\widetilde{f}|_{E_{Z}}:E_{Z}\rightarrow E_{f(Z)}
is an isomorphism as complex vector spaces and left modules over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
for all
Z\in\Omega_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

5.2.2.
Transition data for nc vector bundles

Suppose
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
is a nc holomorphic vector bundle of rank
r
embedded in
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
. From the definition above (Definition
5.10
) it follows that there exists an open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
of
\Omega
by uniformly open subsets that trivialize the bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
. That is, for each
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
, there exists a nc uniformly bi-holomorphic mapping

\varphi_{\alpha}:E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})

such that
\pi\circ\varphi_{\alpha}=\pi
on
E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}
. Furthermore, the restriction

\varphi_{\alpha}|_{E_{Z}}:E_{Z}\to\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})_{Z}

to the corresponding fibres over
Z
is an isomorphism as complex vector spaces as well as of left modules over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
Z\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda
, the transition map

\varphi_{\alpha\beta}:=\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\beta}^{-1}:\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})\rightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r}),

is a nc uniformly bi-holomorphic mapping satisfying
\pi_{1}\circ\varphi_{\alpha\beta}=\pi_{1}
, which ensures that
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
preserves the fibres, mapping

\{Z\}\times({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})^{r}\to\{Z\}\times({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})^{r},

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
Z\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta})\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
. Moreover, the restriction of
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
to each fibre induces an isomorphism of vector spaces and free left modules. When
Z\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta})\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
, such an isomorphism necessarily takes the form

(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))\mapsto\left(Z,\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\end{bmatrix}g_{\alpha\beta}(Z)\right)\quad\text{for all}\quad A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}

for a uniquely determined
g_{\alpha\beta}(Z)\in\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})
, where
g_{\alpha\beta}(Z)
is viewed as
r\times r
block matrix with
n\times n
blocks acting via right matrix multiplication. This shows that the transition map
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
is explicitly given by

(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))\overset{\varphi_{\alpha\beta}}{\longmapsto}\left(Z,\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\end{bmatrix}g_{\alpha\beta}(Z)\right),

for
Z\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta})_{n}
,
A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The map
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
is thus entirely determined by

g_{\alpha\beta}:\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}\rightarrow\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}}).

Since
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}
is nc uniformly bi-holomorphic mapping, it follows that
g_{\alpha\beta}
is uniformly analytic nc mapping. We refer to these nc mappings as
transition mappings
.

Given a nc holomorphic vector bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
of rank
r
with
E\subset\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})
, we thus obtain a cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
of
\Omega
by uniformly open sets and a collection of uniformly analytic nc mappings

\left\{g_{\alpha\beta}:\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}\rightarrow\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})\right\}_{\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda}.

These mappings satisfy following properties:

(i)

g_{\alpha\alpha}\equiv\mathrm{Id}_{nc}
, the nc constant function taking the value as the identity matrix of correct size over
{\mathbb{C}}^{r\times r}
, since
g_{\alpha\alpha}\equiv\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}
is the identity homomorphism of the nc trivial bundle of rank
r
over
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
;

(ii)

g_{\alpha\beta}g_{\beta\alpha}\equiv\mathrm{Id}_{nc}
, since
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}\varphi_{\beta\alpha}
is the identity homomorphism of the nc trivial bundle of rank
r
over
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}
;

(iii)

g_{\alpha\beta}g_{\beta\gamma}g_{\gamma\alpha}\equiv\mathrm{Id}_{nc}
, since
\varphi_{\alpha\beta}\varphi_{\beta\gamma}\varphi_{\gamma\alpha}
is
the identity homomorphism of the nc trivial bundle of rank
r
over
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}
.

Note that the last condition itself derives the other two. So it is enough to have only condition, namely, the last one for getting all of the conditions above. We refer to the last condition – condition (iii) – as
nc (Čech) cocycle condition
for the family
\{g_{\alpha\beta}\}
of uniformly analytic maps relative to the uniformly open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
of
\Omega
. The family of maps
\{g_{\alpha\beta}\}
is then called a
nc cocycle
with values in
\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})
relative to the cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
.

Next, consider the direct summand similarity extension
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}
of the trivial nc holomorphic bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
defined as:

\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}:=\{(X,A)\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d}\times\mathcal{V})_{nc}:(X\oplus Y,A\oplus B)\in\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})~\text{for some}~(Y,B)\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d}\times\mathcal{V})_{nc}\}.

Since
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
is a uniformly open subset of
({\mathbb{C}}^{d}\times\mathcal{V})_{nc}
, so is
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}
(cf.
[
36
, Proposition 9.1]
). The uniformly analytic nc surjection
\pi:\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\rightarrow\Omega
uniquely extends to a uniformly analytic nc surjective mapping (cf.
[
36
, Proposition 9.2 (I), Proposition A.3]
)

\widetilde{\pi}:\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}\rightarrow\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}.

For
Z\in(\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}})_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the fibre
\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}(Z)
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}
both as complex vector space and free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module, yielding the trivial nc vector bundle

\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}\overset{\widetilde{\pi}}{\rightarrow}\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}.

Indeed, for
Z\in(\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}})_{n}
, there exist
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in\Omega_{m}
such that
Z\oplus W\in\Omega_{n+m}
. Consequently, we have the following inclusion:

\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}\oplus\mathcal{V}^{m\times m}\subset\mathcal{V}^{(n+m)\times(n+m)}\simeq\pi^{-1}(Z\oplus W).

This implies that
\{Z\oplus W\}\times(\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}\oplus\mathcal{V}^{m\times m})
is contained in
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
verifying
\{Z\}\times\mathcal{V}^{n\times n}\subset\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}
. We record this discussion in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.13
.

Given the trivial nc holomorphic vector bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
, the direct summand similarity extension
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}
of
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
is the trivial nc holomorphic vector bundle over
\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
. That is,

\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})_{\text{d.s.e}}=\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}}(\mathcal{V})

We now observe in the proposition below that the direct summand similarity extension
E_{\text{d.s.e}}
of the nc subset
E\subset\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
turns out to be a nc holomorphic vector bundle of rank
r
over
\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
which we define to be the
direct summand similarity extension bundle
of the given nc vector bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
.

Proposition 5.14
.

Given a nc holomorphic vector bundle
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
of rank
r
embedded in
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})
, the direct summand similarity extension
E_{\text{d.s.e}}
of
E
is an nc holomorphic vector bundle over
\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
of rank
r
.

Proof.

Since
\pi|_{E}:E\rightarrow\Omega
is surjective, so is the induced mapping
\widetilde{\pi}|_{E_{\text{d.s.e}}}:E_{\text{d.s.e}}\rightarrow\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
. Let
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
be a local trivialization of
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
. By
[
36
, Proposition 9.1, Proposition 9.2]
, the collection
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
becomes an open cover for
\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
and, for each
\alpha\in\Lambda
, the uniformly analytic bijective mapping

\phi_{\alpha}:\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})\to(\pi|_{E})^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})

extends uniquely to the uniformly analytic bijective mapping

\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}}:\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})\to(\widetilde{\pi}|_{E_{\text{d.s.e}}})^{-1}((\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}),

which satisfies
\widetilde{\pi}|_{E_{\text{d.s.e}}}\circ\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}}=\widetilde{\pi}
; here
\widetilde{\pi}
is the canonical projection
\widetilde{\pi}:\mathfrak{T}_{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})\to(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}
.

Moreover, from
\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}}
in
[
36
, Proposition 9.2]
, for any
Z\in(\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}})_{n}
, we have

\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}}\big|_{\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}\{Z\}}\equiv\bigg(\phi_{\alpha}\big|_{\pi^{-1}\{Z\oplus W\}}\bigg)\bigg|_{({\mathbb{C}}^{r})^{n\times n}\times\{0\}},

for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
W\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{m\times m}
. This definition of
\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}}\big|_{\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}\{Z\}}
is independent of the choices of
m
and
W
(see
[
36
, Proposition 9.2]
).

Since
\phi_{\alpha}
is a linear and free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{(n+m)\times(n+m)}
module isomorphism on the fibre
\pi^{-1}\{Z\oplus W\}
,
\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}}
on
\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}\{Z\}
is also a linear and free left
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
module isomorphism. As a consequence,
\{((\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}},\widetilde{\phi_{\alpha}})\}_{\alpha\in\Lambda}
defines a local trivialization of
E_{\text{d.s.e}}
over
\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
, thereby establishing that
E_{\text{d.s.e}}
is a nc vector bundle over
\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
of rank
r
. This completes the proof.
∎

Finally, it can be seen that the equivalence classes of nc holomorphic vector bundles up to isomorphism are preserved under the direct summand similarity extensions. In particular, we have the following result.

Theorem 5.15
.

Two nc holomorphic vector bundles
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
and
F\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
embedded into a trivial nc vector bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
are isomorphism if and only if so are the corresponding direct summand similarity extensions
E_{\text{d.s.e}}\overset{\widetilde{\pi}}{\rightarrow}\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
and
F_{\text{d.s.e }}\overset{\widetilde{\pi}}{\rightarrow}\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}}
of
E
and
F
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
and
F\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
be isomorphic as nc vector bundles. Then there is a bijective nc mapping
f:E\to F
such that both
f
and
f^{-1}
are locally uniformly bounded with respect to the relative uniformly open topologies on
E
and
F
induced from that on
({\mathbb{C}}^{d}\times\mathcal{V})_{nc}
, respectively, and
f
satisfies

\pi\circ f=\pi

along with the property that the restriction
f|_{E_{Z}}:E_{Z}\to F_{Z}
is an isomorphism as complex vector spaces as well as left modules over
{\mathbb{C}}^{m\times m}
for
Z\in\Omega_{m}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Following
[
36
, Proposition 9.2]
, we now define the bijective nc mapping
\widetilde{f}:E_{\text{d.s.e}}\to F_{\text{d.s.e}}
extending the given isomorphism
f:E\to F
such that both
\widetilde{f}
and
\widetilde{f}^{-1}
are uniformly analytic. Evidently,
\widetilde{f}
satisfies the identity

\widetilde{\pi}\circ\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{\pi}.

Finally, for
Z\in(\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}})_{n}
, there exists
W\in({\mathbb{C}}^{d})^{m\times m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
Z\oplus W\in\Omega
. But this implies that
W\in(\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}})_{m}
as well. Consequently, we have from the construction of
E_{\text{d.s.e}}
described above that
E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}\oplus E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{W}\subset E_{Z\oplus W}
where
E_{Z}
and
E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}
are the fibres of
E
and
E_{\text{d.s.e}}
over
Z
, respectively. Moreover, it follows from the definition of
\widetilde{f}
that

\widetilde{f}\big|_{E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}}\equiv(f|_{E_{Z\oplus W}})\big|_{E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}}.

Therefore, the restriction
\widetilde{f}|_{E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}}:E_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}\to F_{\text{d.s.e}}|_{Z}
is an isomorphism as complex vector spaces as well as left modules over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
for
Z\in(\Omega_{\text{d.s.e}})_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

In view of Theorem
5.15
, one can define an equivalence relation on nc cocycles over the same uniformly open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
of
\Omega
by saying
\{g_{\alpha\beta}\}
is
equivalent
or
cohomologous
to another nc cocycle
\{h_{\alpha\beta}\}
if there exist uniformly analytic functions
\lambda_{\alpha}:(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\rightarrow\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})
satisfying

( 5.3 )

\widetilde{g_{\alpha\beta}}(Z)\lambda_{\beta}(Z)=\lambda_{\alpha}(Z)\widetilde{h_{\alpha\beta}}(Z),\quad Z\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{\beta})_{\text{d.s.e}},

for every
\alpha,\beta\in\Lambda
such that
(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{\beta})_{\text{d.s.e}}\neq\emptyset
, where
\{\widetilde{g_{\alpha\beta}}\}
and
\{\widetilde{h_{\alpha\beta}}\}
are nc cocycles with respect to
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\}
obtained by extending the cocycles
\{g_{\alpha\beta}\}
and
\{h_{\alpha\beta}\}
, respectively, to the direct summand similarity extension
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\}
of the cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
. A cocycle
\{g_{\alpha\beta}\}
is said to be cohomologous to the trivial cocycle if there exists uniformly analytic functions
\lambda_{\alpha}:(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\rightarrow\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})
satisfying

\widetilde{g_{\alpha\beta}}(Z)=\lambda_{\alpha}(Z)\circ\lambda_{\beta}(Z)^{-1}\quad\text{for}~~Z\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{\text{d.s.e}}\cap(\mathcal{U}_{\beta})_{\text{d.s.e}}.

It turns out that equivalent nc cocycles give rise to equivalent nc vector bundles, as recorded in the theorem below.

Theorem 5.16
.

Let
\Omega
be a uniformly open nc set which is invariant under similarity and direct summands. Suppose
E\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
and
F\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
are two nc holomorphic vector bundles embedded in the trivial bundle
\mathfrak{T}_{\Omega}(\mathcal{V})\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}\Omega
. Then
E
and
F
are isomorphic if and only if there exists a uniformly open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
– consisting of uniformly open sets invariant under direct summands and similarities – of
\Omega
trivializing both
E
and
F
such that the cocycles associated to the transition functions with respect to this cover are equivalent.

Proof.

Since
\Omega
is invariant under the similarity and direct summands, we may assume, without loss of generality, that the uniformly open sets
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
in any nc trivialization
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha})\}
of a nc vector bundle over
\Omega
are also invariant under these operations.

Indeed, given a local trivialization
\varphi:(\pi|_{E})^{-1}(\mathcal{U})\rightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\mathcal{U}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})
over a uniformly open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\Omega
, we first consider the direct summand extension
\mathcal{U}_{\text{d.s.e}}
of
\mathcal{U}
, followed by the similarity envelop
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{\text{d.s.e}}}
of
\mathcal{U}_{\text{d.s.e}}
. It follows from the proof of
[
36
, Proposition 9.2 (i) and Proposition A.3 ]
that there exists unique trivialization
\widetilde{\varphi_{\text{d.s.e}}}:(\pi|_{E})^{-1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{\text{d.s.e}}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{T}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}_{\text{d.s.e}}}}({\mathbb{C}}^{r})
extending
\varphi
.

Suppose that
E
and
F
are isomorphic nc holomorphic vector bundles. Let
\{\mathcal{U}_{i}\}
and
\{\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\}
be coverings of
\Omega
consisting of uniformly open subsets invariant under direct summands and similarities, which trivialize
E
and
F
, respectively. The common refinement

\mathcal{U}:=\{\mathcal{U}_{i}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}_{j}\}

remains a covering of
\Omega
consisting of uniformly open subsets that are similarly invariant under direct summands and similarities. Furthermore, the restrictions of local trivializations for
E
and
F
to this refinement trivialize them. Denote these nc trivializations for
E
by
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha})\}
and for
F
by
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})\}
.

Since
E
and
F
are isomorphic as nc vector bundles, there is a uniformly bi-holomorphic mapping
f:E\rightarrow F
that preserves the fibres. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider a point
(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})\times({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})^{r}
. Applying
\psi_{\alpha}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}
to it, we obtain the point

\psi_{\alpha}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})\times({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})^{r}.

Because each of these nc maps preserves the fibres and their restrictions to the fibres are free left module (over
{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n}
) isomorphisms, it follows as in the discussion in Subsection
5.2.2
that there exists a uniformly analytic function
\lambda_{\alpha}:\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})
such that

\psi_{\alpha}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))=(Z,\left[\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right]\lambda_{\alpha}(Z)).

Now on a non-empty intersection
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}
, we have (with a slight abuse of notation)

\lambda_{\alpha}(Z)g^{E}_{\alpha\beta}(Z)=\psi_{\alpha}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\alpha}^{-1}\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\beta}^{-1}=\psi_{\alpha}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\beta}^{-1},

and

g^{F}_{\alpha\beta}(Z)\lambda_{\beta}(Z)=\psi_{\alpha}\circ\psi_{\beta}^{-1}\psi_{\beta}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\beta}^{-1}=\psi_{\alpha}\circ f\circ\varphi_{\beta}^{-1}

verifying that the nc cocycles
\{g^{E}_{\alpha\beta}\}
for
E
and
\{g^{F}_{\alpha\beta}\}
for
F
over the same uniformly open cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
of
\Omega
are equivalent.

Conversely, suppose that
E
and
F
have equivalent nc cocycles
\{g^{E}_{\alpha\beta}\}
and
\{g^{F}_{\alpha\beta}\}
of uniformly analytic transition functions over some cover
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
of
\Omega
consisting of direct summands and similarities invariant uniformly open sets trivializing both
E
and
F
. Then by the definition of equivalent cocycles (cf. Equation (
5.3
)), it follows that there exists a family of uniformly analytic functions
\big\{\lambda_{\alpha}:\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\coprod_{n=1}^{\infty}\text{GL}(nr,{\mathbb{C}})\big\}
such that

\lambda_{\alpha}(Z)g^{E}_{\alpha\beta}(Z)=g^{F}_{\alpha\beta}(Z)\lambda_{\beta}(Z),\quad Z\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}.

Let
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\varphi_{\alpha})\}
and
\{(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})\}
be nc trivializations for
E
and
F
, respectively. On each
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
, we consider the mapping
f_{\alpha}:E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}\rightarrow F|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}
defined by

f_{\alpha}:=\psi^{-1}_{\alpha}\circ\lambda_{\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\alpha}.

Here, by the composition with
\lambda_{\alpha}
, we mean the map

(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))\mapsto\big(Z,\begin{bmatrix}A_{1}&\cdots&A_{r}\end{bmatrix}\lambda_{\alpha}(Z)\big)

for
(Z,(A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}))\in(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap{\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})\times({\mathbb{C}}^{n\times n})^{r}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. It thus defines an isomorphism between the nc holomorphic vector bundles
E
and
F
restricted to the uniformly open set
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
.

Note that since the nc cocycles
\{g^{E}_{\alpha\beta}\}
and
\{g^{F}_{\alpha\beta}\}
are equivalent via
\{\lambda_{\alpha}\}
,
f_{\alpha}
agrees with
f_{\beta}
on
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\beta}
, whenever it is non-empty. Consequently, the association
f:E\rightarrow F
given by

f|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}.:=f_{\alpha}

defines a well defined function on
E
. We now show that
f
is a nc function.

Evidently,
f
is graded. Let
P\in E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}
and
Q\in E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\beta}}
. Assume that
P\oplus Q\in E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}}
. Because the uniformly open sets
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
are invariant under direct summands and similarities and
\pi
is a nc function, so are the uniformly open sets
\{E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}=\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})\}
. Therefore, both
P
and
Q
are in
E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}}
. Consequently,

f(P\oplus Q)=f_{\gamma}(P\oplus Q)=f_{\gamma}(P)\oplus f_{\gamma}(Q)=f_{\alpha}(P)\oplus f_{\beta}(Q)=f(P)\oplus f(Q)

implying that
f
respects the direct sums. Since each
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
is invariant under similarities and each
f_{\alpha}
is a nc function, it follows that
f
respects the similarities as well. Furthermore, since each
f_{\alpha}:E|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}\rightarrow F|_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}
is an isomorphism of nc holomorphic vector bundles, so is the nc function
f
.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.
